## original-en · darg · 11:6

Thank Heaven, I remained but a short time in this distressed situation. I was relieved from it by the humane hand of _Mr. David Ruggles_, whose vigilance, kindness, and perseverance, I shall never forget. I am glad of an opportunity to express, as far as words can, the love and gratitude I bear him. Mr. Ruggles is now afflicted with blindness, and is himself in need of the same kind offices which he was once so forward in the performance of toward others. I had been in New York but a few days, when Mr. Ruggles sought me out, and very kindly took me to his boarding-house at the corner of Church and Lespenard Streets. Mr. Ruggles was then very deeply engaged in the memorable _Darg_ case, as well as attending to a number of other fugitive slaves, devising ways and means for their successful escape; and, though watched and hemmed in on almost every side, he seemed to be more than a match for his enemies.

The enslaver involved in the New York case mentioned in connection with David Ruggles.

## modern-en · douglass · 1:0

I was born in Tuckahoe, near Hillsborough, about twelve miles from Easton, in Talbot County, Maryland. I have no reliable knowledge of my own age, since I have never seen any official record of it. Most slaves know as little about their ages as horses do about theirs, and nearly every master I knew of wanted to keep them that ignorant. I cannot remember ever meeting a slave who could name his birthday. The closest they ever come is something like planting-time, harvest-time, cherry-time, spring, or fall. Not knowing my own age troubled me even as a child. The white children could state theirs, and I could not see why I should be denied the same privilege. I was not allowed to ask my master about it. He considered any such question from a slave improper, impertinent, and a sign of a restless spirit. My nearest estimate puts me now at between twenty-seven and twenty-eight, which I reach because I once heard my master say, sometime in 1835, that I was about seventeen.

The author and narrator, recounting his life under slavery.

## modern-en · harriet · 1:1

My mother's name was Harriet Bailey. She was the daughter of Isaac and Betsey Bailey, both of them colored and quite dark. Her complexion was darker than either my grandmother's or my grandfather's.

Douglass’s mother, enslaved and hired out away from her son.

## modern-en · betsey · 1:1

## modern-en · stewart · 1:3

In my whole life I saw my mother, knowing her as my mother, no more than four or five times, and each of those visits was very brief and came at night. She had been hired out to a Mr. Stewart, who lived about twelve miles from my home. She made her visits to me at night, walking the entire distance on foot after finishing a full day's labor. She was a field hand, and any slave not in the field by sunrise was whipped for it, unless granted special permission by the master—a permission rarely given, and one that earned whoever granted it the flattering name of a kind master. I do not recall ever seeing my mother in daylight. She was only with me at night. She would lie down with me and get me to sleep, but long before I woke she was gone. Very little ever passed between us in words. Death soon ended the little we could have while she lived, and ended her hardship and suffering along with it. She died when I was about seven, on one of my master's farms near Lee's Mill. I was not allowed to be present during her illness, at her death, or at her burial. She was gone long before I learned anything of it. Having never really known her soothing presence or her tender, watchful care, I received the news of her death with much the same feeling I would have had at the death of a stranger.

The man to whom Douglass’s mother is hired out.

## modern-en · anthony · 1:7

I have had two masters. The first was named Anthony—I do not remember his first name. He was usually called Captain Anthony, a title he had acquired, I assume, by sailing a craft on the Chesapeake Bay. He was not considered a wealthy slaveholder. He owned two or three farms and about thirty slaves, all under the charge of an overseer named Plummer. Mr. Plummer was a miserable drunkard, a foul-mouthed swearer, and a savage brute. He always went armed with a cowskin whip and a heavy club. I have known him to cut and slash the women's heads so horribly that even my master would grow enraged at his cruelty and threaten to whip him if he did not control himself. My master, though, was no humane slaveholder. It took extraordinary barbarity on an overseer's part to disturb him. He was a cruel man, hardened by a long life of slaveholding. At times he seemed to take real pleasure in whipping a slave. I was often awakened at daybreak by the most heart-rending screams of an aunt of mine, whom he used to tie up to a joist and whip across her bare back until she was literally covered in blood. No words, no tears, no prayers from his bloodied victim could move his iron heart from its bloody purpose. The louder she screamed, the harder he whipped; and where the blood ran fastest, there he whipped longest. He would whip her to make her scream and whip her to make her hush, and only when overcome by exhaustion would he stop swinging the blood-clotted cowskin. I remember the first time I ever witnessed this horrible exhibition. I was only a child, but I remember it well, and I will never forget it as long as I remember anything. It was the first of a long series of such outrages that I was doomed to witness and to take part in. It struck me with terrible force. It was the blood-stained gate, the entrance into the hell of slavery, through which I was about to pass. It was a most terrible spectacle, and I wish I could put on paper the feelings with which I watched it.

Douglass’s first enslaver, who manages Colonel Lloyd’s plantations.

## modern-en · hester · 1:8

This took place very soon after I went to live with my old master, and it came about as follows. Aunt Hester had gone out one night—where or for what, I do not know—and happened to be away when my master wanted her. He had ordered her not to go out in the evenings, and had warned her that she must never let him catch her in the company of a certain young man who was courting her, a slave belonging to Colonel Lloyd. The young man's name was Ned Roberts, generally called Lloyd's Ned. Why my master was so possessive of her may safely be left to the imagination. She was a woman of noble figure and graceful proportions, with very few equals and even fewer superiors in appearance among the women, colored or white, of our neighborhood.

Douglass’s aunt, enslaved in Captain Anthony’s household.

## modern-en · andrew · 2:0

My master’s family consisted of two sons, Andrew and Richard; one daughter, Lucretia; and her husband, Captain Thomas Auld. They all lived together in one house, on the home plantation of Colonel Edward Lloyd. My master was Colonel Lloyd’s clerk and superintendent—what you might call the overseer of the overseers. I spent two years of my childhood on this plantation, in my old master’s family. It was here that I witnessed the bloody event I described in the first chapter; and since this plantation was where I formed my first impressions of slavery, I will describe it, and describe slavery as it existed there. The plantation lies about twelve miles north of Easton, in Talbot county, on the border of Miles River. Its main crops were tobacco, corn, and wheat. These were grown in great abundance, so that, with the produce of this and the other farms he owned, my master was able to keep a large sloop almost constantly busy carrying them to market in Baltimore. This sloop was named Sally Lloyd, in honor of one of the colonel’s daughters. My master’s son-in-law, Captain Auld, was the captain of the vessel, which was otherwise crewed by the colonel’s own slaves. Their names were Peter, Isaac, Rich, and Jake. The other slaves regarded them very highly and looked upon them as the privileged ones of the plantation; for in the eyes of the slaves it was no small thing to be allowed to see Baltimore.

Captain Anthony’s son and Lucretia’s brother.

## modern-en · willis · 2:1

Colonel Lloyd kept three to four hundred slaves on his home plantation, and owned a great many more on the neighboring farms that belonged to him. The farms nearest to the home plantation were named Wye Town and New Design. “Wye Town” was under the supervision of a man named Noah Willis. New Design was under the supervision of a Mr. Townsend. The overseers of these and all the other farms—more than twenty in all—took their advice and direction from the managers of the home plantation. This was the great center of business. It was the seat of government for all twenty farms. Every dispute among the overseers was settled here. If a slave was convicted of any serious offense, became unmanageable, or showed a determination to run away, he was brought here at once, severely whipped, put aboard the sloop, carried to Baltimore, and sold to Austin Woolfolk, or to some other slave-trader, as a warning to the slaves who remained.

The overseer at Colonel Lloyd’s Wye Town plantation.

## modern-en · severe · 2:3

The slaves were given no beds, unless one coarse blanket can be counted as a bed, and only the men and women received even those. This, however, is not considered a very great hardship. They suffer less from the lack of beds than from the lack of time to sleep; for when their day’s work in the field is finished, most of them still have their washing, mending, and cooking to do, and have few or none of the ordinary means of doing any of it, so that many of their sleeping hours are spent preparing for the next day in the field. When this is done, old and young, male and female, married and single drop down side by side on one common bed—the cold, damp floor—each covering himself or herself with their miserable blankets; and there they sleep until they are summoned to the field by the driver’s horn. At its sound, all must rise and be off to the field. There can be no lingering; everyone must be at his or her post; and woe to those who do not hear this morning summons to the field. For if they are not awakened by the sense of hearing, they are awakened by the sense of feeling: no age and no sex is shown any mercy. Mr. Severe, the overseer, used to stand by the door of the slave quarters, armed with a large hickory stick and a heavy cowskin whip, ready to whip anyone unlucky enough not to hear, or who for any other reason failed to be ready to start for the field at the sound of the horn.

The overseer at Colonel Lloyd’s home plantation before Mr. Hopkins.

## modern-en · hopkins-overseer · 2:5

Mr. Severe’s place was filled by a Mr. Hopkins. He was a very different man. He was less cruel, less profane, and made less noise than Mr. Severe. His conduct showed no extraordinary displays of cruelty. He whipped, but he seemed to take no pleasure in it. The slaves called him a good overseer.

The overseer who succeeds Mr. Severe at Colonel Lloyd’s home plantation.

## modern-en · mdurmond · 3:0

Colonel Lloyd kept a large, beautifully tended garden that gave nearly full-time work to four men besides the head gardener, Mr. M’Durmond. It was probably the greatest attraction on the place. All through the summer, people traveled from far and wide—from Baltimore, Easton, and Annapolis—just to see it. It was full of fruit of almost every kind, from the hardy northern apple to the delicate southern orange. The garden was also no small source of trouble on the plantation. Its fine fruit was a powerful temptation to the hungry swarms of boys and to the older slaves alike, few of whom had either the virtue or the vice to resist it. Hardly a day went by in summer without some slave taking the lash for stealing fruit. The colonel tried every kind of trick to keep his slaves out of it. The last and most effective was to tar the fence all the way around; after that, if a slave was caught with any tar on him, it was taken as proof enough that he had been in the garden or had tried to get in—and either way the head gardener whipped him severely. The scheme worked: the slaves grew as afraid of tar as of the lash, and seemed to understand that there was no touching tar without being marked by it.

Colonel Lloyd’s chief gardener.

## modern-en · barney-old · 3:2

Two slaves looked after all of this—old Barney and young Barney, father and son. It was their only job, but it was far from easy, because Colonel Lloyd was more particular about the care of his horses than about anything else. The slightest bit of neglect was unforgivable and brought the harshest punishment down on whoever was responsible. No excuse could protect them if the colonel so much as suspected that his horses had been neglected—and he suspected it often, which made the two Barneys' position a miserable one. They never knew when they were safe from punishment. They were frequently whipped when they least deserved it and escaped when they deserved it most. Everything turned on how the horses looked and on the colonel's own mood when they were brought to him for use. If a horse did not move quickly enough or hold its head high enough, the blame fell on its keepers. It was painful to stand near the stable door and listen to the stream of complaints whenever a horse was led out: “This horse has not had proper attention. He has not been sufficiently rubbed and curried, or he has not been properly fed; his food was too wet or too dry; he got it too soon or too late; he was too hot or too cold; he had too much hay, and not enough of grain; or he had too much grain, and not enough of hay; instead of old Barney's attending to the horse, he had very improperly left it to his son.” To every one of these complaints, however unfair, the slave had to answer not a single word. Colonel Lloyd would not tolerate the least contradiction from a slave; when he spoke, a slave had to stand, listen, and tremble—and that is literally how it was. I have seen Colonel Lloyd force old Barney, a man between fifty and sixty years old, to bare his bald head, kneel on the cold, damp ground, and take more than thirty lashes at a time across his naked, work-worn shoulders. Colonel Lloyd had three sons—Edward, Murray, and Daniel—and three sons-in-law, Mr. Winder, Mr. Nicholson, and Mr. Lowndes. All of them lived at the Great House Farm and enjoyed the privilege of whipping the servants whenever they pleased, from old Barney down to William Wilkes, the coach-driver. I have seen Winder make one of the house-servants stand just far enough off to be reached with the tip of his whip, and raise great welts on the man's back with every stroke.

An enslaved stable keeper on Colonel Lloyd’s plantation; young Barney’s father.

An enslaved stable keeper on Colonel Lloyd’s plantation; old Barney’s son.

## modern-en · edward-lloyd · 3:2

Two slaves looked after all of this—old Barney and young Barney, father and son. It was their only job, but it was far from easy, because Colonel Lloyd was more particular about the care of his horses than about anything else. The slightest bit of neglect was unforgivable and brought the harshest punishment down on whoever was responsible. No excuse could protect them if the colonel so much as suspected that his horses had been neglected—and he suspected it often, which made the two Barneys' position a miserable one. They never knew when they were safe from punishment. They were frequently whipped when they least deserved it and escaped when they deserved it most. Everything turned on how the horses looked and on the colonel's own mood when they were brought to him for use. If a horse did not move quickly enough or hold its head high enough, the blame fell on its keepers. It was painful to stand near the stable door and listen to the stream of complaints whenever a horse was led out: “This horse has not had proper attention. He has not been sufficiently rubbed and curried, or he has not been properly fed; his food was too wet or too dry; he got it too soon or too late; he was too hot or too cold; he had too much hay, and not enough of grain; or he had too much grain, and not enough of hay; instead of old Barney's attending to the horse, he had very improperly left it to his son.” To every one of these complaints, however unfair, the slave had to answer not a single word. Colonel Lloyd would not tolerate the least contradiction from a slave; when he spoke, a slave had to stand, listen, and tremble—and that is literally how it was. I have seen Colonel Lloyd force old Barney, a man between fifty and sixty years old, to bare his bald head, kneel on the cold, damp ground, and take more than thirty lashes at a time across his naked, work-worn shoulders. Colonel Lloyd had three sons—Edward, Murray, and Daniel—and three sons-in-law, Mr. Winder, Mr. Nicholson, and Mr. Lowndes. All of them lived at the Great House Farm and enjoyed the privilege of whipping the servants whenever they pleased, from old Barney down to William Wilkes, the coach-driver. I have seen Winder make one of the house-servants stand just far enough off to be reached with the tip of his whip, and raise great welts on the man's back with every stroke.

One of Colonel Lloyd’s sons.

## modern-en · jepson · 3:5

It is partly because of facts like these that slaves, when asked about their condition and the character of their masters, almost always say they are content and that their masters are kind. Slaveholders have been known to plant spies among their slaves to find out what they really think and feel about their lot. This has happened often enough that the slaves have adopted the maxim that a still tongue makes a wise head. They keep the truth to themselves rather than face the consequences of speaking it—and in doing so they prove themselves a part of the human family. Whatever they have to say about their masters is usually in the master's favor, especially when they are speaking to a stranger they cannot yet trust. As a slave I was often asked whether I had a kind master, and I don't recall ever once saying no; nor did I feel, in answering that way, that I was telling an outright lie, because I always measured my master's kindness against the standard of kindness set by the slaveholders around us. What is more, slaves are like everyone else and absorb the same prejudices others do. They think their own is better than anyone else's. Many, under the spell of this prejudice, believe their own masters are better than other people's masters—sometimes even when the very reverse is true. It is not unusual for slaves to fall out and quarrel among themselves over whose master is the better man, each insisting on the superior goodness of his own over the rest. And yet, taken one by one, they curse their masters all the same. So it was on our plantation. Whenever Colonel Lloyd's slaves met the slaves of Jacob Jepson, they seldom parted without a quarrel over their masters—Lloyd's slaves arguing that he was the richest, and Mr. Jepson's that he was the smarter and more of a man. Lloyd's slaves would boast that he could buy and sell Jacob Jepson; Jepson's slaves would boast that their master could whip Colonel Lloyd. These arguments almost always ended in a fight between the two sides, and whoever won the fight was supposed to have won the point at issue. They seemed to think that their masters' greatness somehow passed on to them. It was bad enough to be a slave at all; but to be a poor man's slave was held to be a disgrace indeed!

A neighboring enslaver whose people argue with Colonel Lloyd’s over their masters’ wealth.

## modern-en · gore · 4:0

Mr. Hopkins stayed only a short while in the position of overseer. I do not know why his time there was so brief, but I suspect he lacked the harshness that would have satisfied Colonel Lloyd. Mr. Hopkins was followed by Mr. Austin Gore, a man who possessed, to a remarkable degree, all the qualities considered essential to what people call a first-rate overseer. Mr. Gore had already served Colonel Lloyd as overseer on one of the out-farms, and he had proven himself worthy of the higher post of overseer on the home plantation, the Great House Farm.

The overseer who succeeds Mr. Hopkins at Colonel Lloyd’s home plantation.

## modern-en · demby · 4:3

His savage brutality was matched only by the utter coolness with which he carried out the grossest and most savage acts against the slaves in his charge. Mr. Gore once set out to whip one of Colonel Lloyd’s slaves, a man named Demby. He had given Demby only a few lashes when, to escape the beating, Demby ran and plunged into a creek, standing there with the water up to his shoulders and refusing to come out. Mr. Gore told him that he would call him three times, and that if he did not come out by the third call, he would shoot him. The first call was given. Demby made no reply but held his ground. The second and third calls were given, with the same result. Then Mr. Gore, without consulting or conferring with anyone, and without even giving Demby an extra call, raised his musket to his face, took deadly aim at his standing victim, and in an instant poor Demby was gone. His mangled body sank out of sight, and blood and brains stained the water where he had stood.

A man enslaved by Colonel Lloyd and subject to overseer Austin Gore.

## modern-en · lanman · 4:5

I weigh my words carefully when I say this—that killing a slave, or any colored person, in Talbot county, Maryland, is not treated as a crime, either by the courts or by the community. Mr. Thomas Lanman, of St. Michael’s, killed two slaves, one of whom he killed with a hatchet by beating his brains out. He used to brag about committing that awful and bloody deed. I have heard him do it while laughing, saying, among other things, that he was the only one in the neighborhood doing his country any good, and that when others did as much as he had done, we should be rid of “the d——d niggers.”

A white resident of St. Michael’s whom Douglass names in his account of violence against enslaved people.

## modern-en · giles · 4:6

The wife of Mr. Giles Hicks, who lived only a short distance from where I used to live, murdered my wife’s cousin, a young girl between fifteen and sixteen years of age, mangling her body in the most horrible way, breaking her nose and her breastbone with a stick, so that the poor girl died a few hours later. She was buried at once, but had been in her untimely grave only a few hours before she was dug up and examined by the coroner, who ruled that she had died from a severe beating. The offense for which this girl was murdered was this: she had been put in charge that night of minding Mrs. Hicks’s baby, and during the night she fell asleep, and the baby cried. Having lost her sleep for several nights before, she did not hear the crying. They were both in the room with Mrs. Hicks. Mrs. Hicks, finding the girl slow to get up, leapt from her bed, snatched an oak stick of wood from the fireplace, and with it broke the girl’s nose and breastbone, and so ended her life. I will not say that this most horrible murder produced no stir in the community. It did produce a stir, but not enough to bring the murderess to punishment. A warrant was issued for her arrest, but it was never carried out. So she escaped not only punishment, but even the discomfort of being brought before a court for her horrible crime.

The husband of the woman who enslaves Anna’s young cousin.

## modern-en · bondly · 4:8

Colonel Lloyd’s slaves were in the habit of spending part of their nights and their Sundays fishing for oysters, and in this way they made up for the shortfall in their meager rations. An old man belonging to Colonel Lloyd, while doing this, happened to stray beyond the boundaries of Colonel Lloyd’s land and onto the property of Mr. Beal Bondly. Mr. Bondly took offense at this trespass, and coming down to the shore with his musket, he fired its deadly charge into the poor old man.

The enslaver whose property borders Colonel Lloyd’s.

## modern-en · hugh · 5:3

I was probably between seven and eight years old when I left Colonel Lloyd’s plantation. I left it with joy. I will never forget the ecstasy with which I received the news that my old master, Anthony, had decided to let me go to Baltimore to live with Mr. Hugh Auld, brother to my old master’s son-in-law, Captain Thomas Auld. I got this news about three days before I left. They were three of the happiest days I ever knew. I spent most of those three days in the creek, washing off the grime of the plantation and getting myself ready to go.

Thomas Auld’s brother and Sophia’s husband, in whose Baltimore household Douglass is enslaved.

## modern-en · sophia · 5:9

Mr. and Mrs. Auld were both at home, and they met me at the door with their little son Thomas, whom I had been given to look after. And there I saw what I had never seen before—a white face glowing with the kindest feelings; it was the face of my new mistress, Sophia Auld. I wish I could describe the rapture that flashed through my soul as I beheld it. It was a new and strange sight to me, lighting up my path with the glow of happiness. Little Thomas was told that here was his Freddy—and I was told to take care of little Thomas. And so I took up the duties of my new home with the most cheering prospect ahead of me.

Hugh Auld’s wife and little Thomas’s mother, in whose Baltimore household Douglass lives.

## modern-en · little-thomas · 5:9

Mr. and Mrs. Auld were both at home, and they met me at the door with their little son Thomas, whom I had been given to look after. And there I saw what I had never seen before—a white face glowing with the kindest feelings; it was the face of my new mistress, Sophia Auld. I wish I could describe the rapture that flashed through my soul as I beheld it. It was a new and strange sight to me, lighting up my path with the glow of happiness. Little Thomas was told that here was his Freddy—and I was told to take care of little Thomas. And so I took up the duties of my new home with the most cheering prospect ahead of me.

Hugh and Sophia Auld’s young son, whom Douglass is sent to look after.

## modern-en · cousin-tom · 5:5

The bonds that usually tie children to their homes were all loosened in my case. I found nothing hard about leaving. My home held no charm; it was not a home to me; and in parting from it I could not feel that I was leaving behind anything I might have enjoyed by staying. My mother was dead, and my grandmother lived far away, so that I rarely saw her. I had two sisters and one brother who lived in the same house with me, but our early separation from our mother had nearly erased the fact of our kinship from our memories. I looked for a home elsewhere, certain that I would find none I liked less than the one I was leaving. And even if my new home brought hardship, hunger, whipping, and nakedness, I had the consolation that I could not have escaped a single one of them by staying. Having already tasted more than enough of them in my old master’s house, and having endured them there, I naturally concluded that I could endure them anywhere else—and especially in Baltimore. For I felt about Baltimore something like the spirit of the proverb that “being hanged in England is preferable to dying a natural death in Ireland.” I had the strongest desire to see Baltimore. Cousin Tom, though not a fluent speaker, had stirred that desire in me by his eloquent descriptions of the place. I could never point to anything at the Great House, however beautiful or grand, without his having seen something at Baltimore that far surpassed it in both beauty and strength. Even the Great House itself, with all its pictures, was far inferior to many of the buildings in Baltimore. So strong was my desire that I believed satisfying it would fully make up for whatever comforts I lost in the exchange. I left without a single regret, and with the highest hopes for future happiness.

Douglass’s cousin, whose descriptions of Baltimore make him eager to see it.

## modern-en · curtis · 5:8

We arrived at Baltimore early on Sunday morning, landing at Smith’s Wharf, not far from Bowley’s Wharf. We had a large flock of sheep on board the sloop, and after helping drive them to Mr. Curtis’s slaughterhouse on Louden Slater’s Hill, I was led by Rich, one of the hands belonging to the sloop, to my new home on Alliciana Street, near Mr. Gardner’s ship-yard, on Fells Point.

The Baltimore slaughterhouse proprietor to whom a cargo of sheep is delivered.

## modern-en · gardner · 5:8

We arrived at Baltimore early on Sunday morning, landing at Smith’s Wharf, not far from Bowley’s Wharf. We had a large flock of sheep on board the sloop, and after helping drive them to Mr. Curtis’s slaughterhouse on Louden Slater’s Hill, I was led by Rich, one of the hands belonging to the sloop, to my new home on Alliciana Street, near Mr. Gardner’s ship-yard, on Fells Point.

The Baltimore shipbuilder who runs the shipyard on Fells Point.

## modern-en · thomas-hamilton · 6:3

I had lived in Baltimore only a short time before I noticed a striking difference in how slaves were treated compared with what I had seen in the country. A city slave is almost a free man compared with a slave on the plantation. He is much better fed and clothed, and he enjoys privileges entirely unknown to the slave on the plantation. There is a trace of decency, a sense of shame, that does much to restrain and check the outbursts of atrocious cruelty so commonly carried out on the plantation. A slaveholder must be desperate indeed to shock the humanity of his non-slaveholding neighbors with the cries of his torn and bleeding slave. Few are willing to take on the disgrace attached to being known as a cruel master; and above all, they would not want to be known as someone who fails to give a slave enough to eat. Every city slaveholder is anxious to be known for feeding his slaves well; and it is only fair to them to say that most of them do give their slaves enough to eat. There are, however, some painful exceptions to this rule. Directly across from us, on Philpot Street, lived Mr. Thomas Hamilton. He owned two slaves. Their names were Henrietta and Mary. Henrietta was about twenty-two years old, Mary about fourteen; and of all the mangled and emaciated creatures I have ever seen, these two were the worst. Anyone who could look on them unmoved must have a heart harder than stone. Mary’s head, neck, and shoulders were literally cut to pieces. I have often felt her head and found it nearly covered with festering sores, caused by the lash of her cruel mistress. I do not know whether her master ever whipped her, but I was an eyewitness to the cruelty of Mrs. Hamilton. I used to be in Mr. Hamilton’s house nearly every day. Mrs. Hamilton would sit in a large chair in the middle of the room, with a heavy cowskin whip always at her side, and scarcely an hour passed during the day without the blood of one of these slaves being drawn. The girls seldom passed her without her saying, “Move faster, you _black gip!_”—and at the same time striking them with the cowskin over the head or shoulders, often drawing blood. She would then say, “Take that, you _black gip!_” and go on, “If you don’t move faster, I’ll move you!” On top of the cruel lashings these slaves endured, they were kept nearly half-starved. They rarely knew what it was to eat a full meal. I have seen Mary fighting with the pigs over the scraps thrown into the street. Mary was kicked and cut to pieces so badly that she was more often called “_pecked_” than by her name.

The Baltimore enslaver whose household includes Henrietta and Mary.

## modern-en · mrs-hamilton · 6:3

I had lived in Baltimore only a short time before I noticed a striking difference in how slaves were treated compared with what I had seen in the country. A city slave is almost a free man compared with a slave on the plantation. He is much better fed and clothed, and he enjoys privileges entirely unknown to the slave on the plantation. There is a trace of decency, a sense of shame, that does much to restrain and check the outbursts of atrocious cruelty so commonly carried out on the plantation. A slaveholder must be desperate indeed to shock the humanity of his non-slaveholding neighbors with the cries of his torn and bleeding slave. Few are willing to take on the disgrace attached to being known as a cruel master; and above all, they would not want to be known as someone who fails to give a slave enough to eat. Every city slaveholder is anxious to be known for feeding his slaves well; and it is only fair to them to say that most of them do give their slaves enough to eat. There are, however, some painful exceptions to this rule. Directly across from us, on Philpot Street, lived Mr. Thomas Hamilton. He owned two slaves. Their names were Henrietta and Mary. Henrietta was about twenty-two years old, Mary about fourteen; and of all the mangled and emaciated creatures I have ever seen, these two were the worst. Anyone who could look on them unmoved must have a heart harder than stone. Mary’s head, neck, and shoulders were literally cut to pieces. I have often felt her head and found it nearly covered with festering sores, caused by the lash of her cruel mistress. I do not know whether her master ever whipped her, but I was an eyewitness to the cruelty of Mrs. Hamilton. I used to be in Mr. Hamilton’s house nearly every day. Mrs. Hamilton would sit in a large chair in the middle of the room, with a heavy cowskin whip always at her side, and scarcely an hour passed during the day without the blood of one of these slaves being drawn. The girls seldom passed her without her saying, “Move faster, you _black gip!_”—and at the same time striking them with the cowskin over the head or shoulders, often drawing blood. She would then say, “Take that, you _black gip!_” and go on, “If you don’t move faster, I’ll move you!” On top of the cruel lashings these slaves endured, they were kept nearly half-starved. They rarely knew what it was to eat a full meal. I have seen Mary fighting with the pigs over the scraps thrown into the street. Mary was kicked and cut to pieces so badly that she was more often called “_pecked_” than by her name.

Thomas Hamilton’s wife, who enslaves Henrietta and Mary.

## modern-en · durgin · 7:3

The plan I adopted, and the one that worked best for me, was to make friends of all the little white boys I met in the street. As many of these as I could, I turned into teachers. With their kindly help, given at different times and in different places, I finally managed to learn to read. Whenever I was sent on errands, I always took my book with me, and by hurrying through one part of an errand, I found time to get a lesson before I returned. I also used to carry bread with me—there was always plenty of it in the house, and I was always welcome to it; for in this respect I was much better off than many of the poor white children in our neighborhood. This bread I gave away to the hungry little urchins, who in return would give me that far more valuable bread of knowledge. I am strongly tempted to give the names of two or three of those little boys, as a testimony to the gratitude and affection I feel for them; but prudence forbids it—not that it would harm me, but it might get them into trouble; for it is almost an unpardonable offense to teach slaves to read in this Christian country. It is enough to say of the dear little fellows that they lived on Philpot Street, very near Durgin and Bailey’s ship-yard. I used to talk this matter of slavery over with them. I would sometimes tell them I wished I could be as free as they would be when they grew to be men. “You will be free as soon as you are twenty-one, _but I am a slave for life!_ Have I not as good a right to be free as you do?” These words used to trouble them; they would show me the keenest sympathy and console me with the hope that something would happen by which I might become free.

One of the proprietors of the shipyard near Douglass’s Baltimore home.

Durgin’s partner in the Baltimore shipyard where Douglass watches carpenters mark timber.

## modern-en · waters · 7:6

I often found myself regretting my own existence and wishing myself dead; and were it not for the hope of being free, I have no doubt that I would have killed myself, or done something for which I would have been killed. While in this state of mind, I was eager to hear anyone speak of slavery. I was a ready listener. Every now and then, I would hear something about the abolitionists. It was some time before I discovered what the word meant. It was always used in such contexts as to make it an intriguing word to me. If a slave ran away and managed to get clear, or if a slave killed his master, set fire to a barn, or did anything that struck a slaveholder as very wrong, it was spoken of as the fruit of _abolition._ Hearing the word in this connection so often, I set out to learn what it meant. The dictionary gave me little or no help. I found that it meant “the act of abolishing;” but then I did not know what was to be abolished. Here I was baffled. I did not dare ask anyone about its meaning, for I was sure it was something they wanted me to know very little about. After waiting patiently, I got hold of one of our city papers, which contained a report of the number of petitions from the north praying for the abolition of slavery in the District of Columbia and of the slave trade between the States. From this time on I understood the words _abolition_ and _abolitionist,_ and I always drew near whenever that word was spoken, expecting to hear something of importance to myself and my fellow-slaves. The light broke in on me little by little. One day I went down to the wharf of Mr. Waters; and seeing two Irishmen unloading a scow of stone, I went over, unasked, and helped them. When we had finished, one of them came up to me and asked if I were a slave. I told him I was. He asked, “Are ye a slave for life?” I told him that I was. The good Irishman seemed deeply moved by my answer. He said to the other that it was a pity so fine a little fellow as myself should be a slave for life. He said it was a shame to hold me. They both advised me to run away to the north; there I would find friends, and there I would be free. I pretended not to be interested in what they said, and acted as if I did not understand them; for I feared they might be treacherous. White men have been known to encourage slaves to escape, and then, to claim the reward, catch them and return them to their masters. I was afraid these seemingly good men might do the same to me; but all the same I remembered their advice, and from that time on I resolved to run away. I looked forward to a time when it would be safe for me to escape. I was too young to think of doing it immediately; besides, I wanted to learn how to write, since I might have need to write my own pass. I comforted myself with the hope that one day I would find a good chance. In the meantime, I would learn to write.

The proprietor of the wharf where Douglass helps two Irish laborers unload stone.

## modern-en · rowe · 8:0

Very soon after I went to live in Baltimore, my old master’s youngest son, Richard, died; and about three years and six months after his death, my old master, Captain Anthony, died as well. He left only his son, Andrew, and his daughter, Lucretia, to share his estate. He died while visiting his daughter at Hillsborough. Cut off so unexpectedly, he left no will directing how his property should be disposed of. It therefore became necessary to have the property appraised, so that it could be divided equally between Mrs. Lucretia and Master Andrew. I was sent for at once, to be valued along with the rest of the property. Once again my whole being rose up in loathing of slavery. I now understood my degraded condition in a new way. Before this, I had grown, if not numb to my situation, at least partly so. I left Baltimore with a young heart weighed down by sadness and a soul full of dread. I took passage with Captain Rowe, in the schooner Wild Cat, and after a sail of about twenty-four hours I found myself near the place where I was born. I had now been away from it for nearly, if not quite, five years. Even so, I remembered the place very well. I had been only about five years old when I left it to go and live with my old master on Colonel Lloyd’s plantation, so that I was now between ten and eleven years old.

The captain of the schooner Wild Cat, on which Douglass travels back to be valued with Anthony’s estate.

## modern-en · amanda · 8:5

Very soon after my return to Baltimore, my mistress, Lucretia, died, leaving behind her husband and one child, Amanda; and a very short time after her death, Master Andrew died as well. Now all the property of my old master, slaves included, was in the hands of strangers—strangers who had had nothing to do with building it up. Not a single slave was set free. All remained slaves, from the youngest to the oldest. If any one thing in my experience, more than any other, served to deepen my conviction of the hellish character of slavery, and to fill me with unspeakable loathing of slaveholders, it was their base ingratitude toward my poor old grandmother. She had served my old master faithfully from her youth into old age. She had been the source of all his wealth; she had peopled his plantation with slaves; she had become a great-grandmother in his service. She had rocked him in infancy, attended him in childhood, served him throughout his life, and at his death wiped the cold death-sweat from his icy brow and closed his eyes forever. Yet she was still left a slave—a slave for life—a slave in the hands of strangers; and in their hands she watched her children, her grandchildren, and her great-grandchildren divided up, like so many sheep, without being granted even the small privilege of a single word about their fate or her own. And to crown their base ingratitude and fiendish cruelty, my grandmother, who was now very old, having outlived my old master and all his children, having seen the beginning and the end of every one of them—when her present owners found that she was of little value, her frame already racked with the pains of old age and complete helplessness rapidly overtaking her once active limbs, they carried her off to the woods, built her a little hut, put up a small mud chimney, and then graciously left her to support herself there in utter loneliness; thus, in effect, turning her out to die! If my poor old grandmother is still alive, she lives to suffer in total loneliness; she lives to remember and mourn the loss of her children, the loss of her grandchildren, and the loss of her great-grandchildren. They are, in the words of the slave’s poet, Whittier,—

Thomas and Lucretia Auld’s daughter.

## modern-en · rowena · 8:8

About two years after the death of Mrs. Lucretia, Master Thomas married his second wife. Her name was Rowena Hamilton. She was the eldest daughter of Mr. William Hamilton. Master now lived in St. Michael’s. Not long after his marriage, a falling-out arose between him and Master Hugh; and as a way of punishing his brother, he took me away from him to live with himself at St. Michael’s. Here I went through another deeply painful separation. It was not, however, as severe as the one I had dreaded at the division of the property; for in the meantime a great change had come over Master Hugh and his once kind and affectionate wife. The effect of brandy on him, and of slavery on her, had brought about a disastrous change in the character of both, so that, as far as they were concerned, I felt I had little to lose by the move. But it was not to them that I was attached. It was to those little Baltimore boys that I felt the strongest attachment. I had received many good lessons from them, and was still receiving them, and the thought of leaving them was painful indeed. I was leaving, too, with no hope of ever being allowed to return. Master Thomas had said he would never let me come back. The rift between himself and his brother he considered impossible to cross.

Thomas Auld’s second wife, born Rowena Hamilton; William Hamilton’s daughter.

## modern-en · dodson · 8:10

I sailed from Baltimore for St. Michael’s in the sloop Amanda, Captain Edward Dodson. During the voyage, I paid close attention to the route the steamboats took on their way to Philadelphia. I found that, instead of heading down the bay, once they reached North Point they went up it, in a north-easterly direction. I considered this knowledge to be of the utmost importance. My determination to run away was revived once more. I resolved to wait only until a favorable opportunity presented itself. When that came, I was determined to be gone.

The captain of the sloop Amanda, which takes Douglass from Baltimore to St. Michael’s.

## modern-en · eliza · 9:0

I have now reached a period of my life when I can give exact dates. I left Baltimore and went to live with Master Thomas Auld at St. Michael’s in March, 1832. By then it had been more than seven years since I had lived with him in the household of my old master, on Colonel Lloyd’s plantation. As a result, we were now nearly complete strangers to each other. He was a new master to me, and I was a new slave to him. I knew nothing of his temper or disposition, and he knew just as little of mine. A very short time, however, brought us into full knowledge of each other. I came to know his wife just as well as I came to know him. They were well matched, for they were equally mean and cruel. Now, for the first time in more than seven years, I was made to feel the painful gnawing of hunger—something I had not experienced since leaving Colonel Lloyd’s plantation. It had been hard enough back then, when I could look back on no period in which I had ever had enough to eat. It was ten times harder now, after living in Master Hugh’s family, where I had always had plenty of food, and good food at that. I have said that Master Thomas was a mean man. He was. Among slaveholders, failing to give a slave enough to eat is regarded as the most extreme form of meanness. The rule is that, no matter how coarse the food may be, there should at least be enough of it. That is the theory, and in the part of Maryland I came from, it is the general practice—though there are many exceptions. Master Thomas gave us enough of neither coarse food nor fine. There were four of us slaves in the kitchen—my sister Eliza, my aunt Priscilla, Henny, and myself—and we were allowed less than half a bushel of corn-meal per week, and very little else, whether meat or vegetables. It was not enough for us to live on. We were therefore reduced to the wretched necessity of surviving at our neighbors’ expense. We did this by begging and stealing, whichever was easier in our time of need, the one being considered just as legitimate as the other. Many times we poor creatures were nearly perishing with hunger while food lay in abundance, rotting in the safe and the smoke-house, and our pious mistress knew it; and yet that mistress and her husband would kneel every morning and pray that God would bless them in basket and store!

Douglass’s sister, enslaved with him in Thomas Auld’s household.

## modern-en · storks · 9:1

As bad as all slaveholders are, we rarely meet one who lacks every element of character that commands respect. My master was one of this rare sort. I do not know of a single noble act he ever performed. The leading trait in his character was meanness, and if there was any other quality in his nature, it was made to serve this one. He was mean, and, like most other mean men, he lacked the ability to hide his meanness. Captain Auld was not born a slaveholder. He had been a poor man, master only of a Bay craft. He came into possession of all his slaves through marriage; and of all men, those who take up slaveholding by adoption are the worst. He was cruel, but cowardly. He gave commands without firmness. In enforcing his rules he was sometimes rigid and sometimes lax. At times he spoke to his slaves with the firmness of Napoleon and the fury of a demon; at other times he might easily have been mistaken for a lost traveler asking directions. He did nothing on his own. He might have passed for a lion, were it not for his ears. In every noble thing he attempted, his own meanness showed through most plainly. His airs, his words, and his actions were the airs, words, and actions of born slaveholders, and, being merely put on, they were awkward enough. He was not even a good imitator. He had every inclination to deceive but not the power. Having no resources within himself, he was forced to copy many others, and being a mere copyist, he was forever the victim of inconsistency; and as a result, he was an object of contempt, even in the eyes of his own slaves. The luxury of having slaves of his own to wait on him was something new to him and something he was unprepared for. He was a slaveholder without the ability to hold slaves. He found himself incapable of managing his slaves by force, by fear, or by fraud. We seldom called him “master;” we generally called him “Captain Auld,” and were hardly inclined to give him any title at all. I have no doubt that our conduct had a great deal to do with making him seem awkward, and therefore irritable. Our lack of reverence for him must have puzzled him greatly. He wanted us to call him master but lacked the firmness needed to command us to do so. His wife used to insist that we call him so, but to no effect. In August, 1832, my master attended a Methodist camp-meeting held in the Bay-side, Talbot county, and there he experienced religion. I held a faint hope that his conversion would lead him to free his slaves, and that, if it did not do that, it would at least make him kinder and more humane. I was disappointed on both counts. It neither made him humane toward his slaves nor moved him to free them. If it had any effect on his character, it made him more cruel and hateful in everything he did; for I believe he was a far worse man after his conversion than before. Before his conversion, he relied on his own depravity to shield and sustain him in his savage barbarity; but after his conversion, he found religious sanction and support for his slaveholding cruelty. He made the loudest claims to piety. His house was a house of prayer. He prayed morning, noon, and night. He very soon distinguished himself among his brethren and was quickly made a class-leader and exhorter. He was very active in revivals, and he proved himself a useful instrument in the church’s hands for converting many souls. His house was a home for the preachers. They used to take great pleasure in coming there to stay; for while he starved us, he stuffed them. We have had three or four preachers there at once. The names of those who came most often while I lived there were Mr. Storks, Mr. Ewery, Mr. Humphry, and Mr. Hickey. I have also seen Mr. George Cookman at our house. We slaves loved Mr. Cookman. We believed him to be a good man. We thought he had been instrumental in getting Mr. Samuel Harrison, a very rich slaveholder, to free his slaves; and somehow we got the impression that he was working to bring about the emancipation of all slaves. Whenever he was at our house, we were sure to be called in to prayers. When the others were there, we were sometimes called in and sometimes not. Mr. Cookman took more notice of us than any of the other ministers. He could not be among us without showing his sympathy for us, and, ignorant as we were, we had the sense to see it.

One of the preachers who stays at Thomas Auld’s house.

## modern-en · ewery · 9:1

As bad as all slaveholders are, we rarely meet one who lacks every element of character that commands respect. My master was one of this rare sort. I do not know of a single noble act he ever performed. The leading trait in his character was meanness, and if there was any other quality in his nature, it was made to serve this one. He was mean, and, like most other mean men, he lacked the ability to hide his meanness. Captain Auld was not born a slaveholder. He had been a poor man, master only of a Bay craft. He came into possession of all his slaves through marriage; and of all men, those who take up slaveholding by adoption are the worst. He was cruel, but cowardly. He gave commands without firmness. In enforcing his rules he was sometimes rigid and sometimes lax. At times he spoke to his slaves with the firmness of Napoleon and the fury of a demon; at other times he might easily have been mistaken for a lost traveler asking directions. He did nothing on his own. He might have passed for a lion, were it not for his ears. In every noble thing he attempted, his own meanness showed through most plainly. His airs, his words, and his actions were the airs, words, and actions of born slaveholders, and, being merely put on, they were awkward enough. He was not even a good imitator. He had every inclination to deceive but not the power. Having no resources within himself, he was forced to copy many others, and being a mere copyist, he was forever the victim of inconsistency; and as a result, he was an object of contempt, even in the eyes of his own slaves. The luxury of having slaves of his own to wait on him was something new to him and something he was unprepared for. He was a slaveholder without the ability to hold slaves. He found himself incapable of managing his slaves by force, by fear, or by fraud. We seldom called him “master;” we generally called him “Captain Auld,” and were hardly inclined to give him any title at all. I have no doubt that our conduct had a great deal to do with making him seem awkward, and therefore irritable. Our lack of reverence for him must have puzzled him greatly. He wanted us to call him master but lacked the firmness needed to command us to do so. His wife used to insist that we call him so, but to no effect. In August, 1832, my master attended a Methodist camp-meeting held in the Bay-side, Talbot county, and there he experienced religion. I held a faint hope that his conversion would lead him to free his slaves, and that, if it did not do that, it would at least make him kinder and more humane. I was disappointed on both counts. It neither made him humane toward his slaves nor moved him to free them. If it had any effect on his character, it made him more cruel and hateful in everything he did; for I believe he was a far worse man after his conversion than before. Before his conversion, he relied on his own depravity to shield and sustain him in his savage barbarity; but after his conversion, he found religious sanction and support for his slaveholding cruelty. He made the loudest claims to piety. His house was a house of prayer. He prayed morning, noon, and night. He very soon distinguished himself among his brethren and was quickly made a class-leader and exhorter. He was very active in revivals, and he proved himself a useful instrument in the church’s hands for converting many souls. His house was a home for the preachers. They used to take great pleasure in coming there to stay; for while he starved us, he stuffed them. We have had three or four preachers there at once. The names of those who came most often while I lived there were Mr. Storks, Mr. Ewery, Mr. Humphry, and Mr. Hickey. I have also seen Mr. George Cookman at our house. We slaves loved Mr. Cookman. We believed him to be a good man. We thought he had been instrumental in getting Mr. Samuel Harrison, a very rich slaveholder, to free his slaves; and somehow we got the impression that he was working to bring about the emancipation of all slaves. Whenever he was at our house, we were sure to be called in to prayers. When the others were there, we were sometimes called in and sometimes not. Mr. Cookman took more notice of us than any of the other ministers. He could not be among us without showing his sympathy for us, and, ignorant as we were, we had the sense to see it.

One of the preachers who stays at Thomas Auld’s house.

## modern-en · wilson · 9:2

While I lived with my master in St. Michael’s, there was a young white man, a Mr. Wilson, who proposed to hold a Sabbath school to teach those slaves who might wish to learn to read the New Testament. We met only three times, when Mr. West and Mr. Fairbanks, both class-leaders, along with many others, descended on us with sticks and other missiles, drove us off, and forbade us to meet again. So ended our little Sabbath school in the pious town of St. Michael’s.

The young white man who begins a Sabbath school for enslaved people.

## modern-en · covey · 9:5

My master and I had quite a number of disagreements. He found me unsuited to his purposes. My life in the city, he said, had had a very harmful effect on me. It had nearly ruined me for every good purpose and prepared me for everything that was bad. One of my greatest faults was letting his horse run away and go down to his father-in-law’s farm, which was about five miles from St. Michael’s. I would then have to go after it. My reason for this kind of carelessness—or carefulness—was that I could always get something to eat when I went there. Master William Hamilton, my master’s father-in-law, always gave his slaves enough to eat. I never left there hungry, no matter how urgent the need for me to hurry back. At last Master Thomas said he would put up with it no longer. I had lived with him nine months, during which he had given me a number of severe whippings, all to no good purpose. He decided to put me out, as he said, to be broken; and for this purpose he hired me out for one year to a man named Edward Covey. Mr. Covey was a poor man, a farm-renter. He rented the place he lived on, as well as the hands he used to work it. Mr. Covey had earned a very high reputation for breaking young slaves, and this reputation was of immense value to him. It allowed him to get his farm worked at far less expense to himself than he could have managed without it. Some slaveholders thought it not much of a loss to let Mr. Covey have their slaves for a year, for the sake of the training they underwent, without any other payment. Because of this reputation, he could hire young help with great ease. On top of Mr. Covey’s natural good qualities, he was a professor of religion—a pious soul—a member and class-leader in the Methodist church. All of this added weight to his reputation as a “nigger-breaker.” I knew all of these facts, having been told them by a young man who had lived there. Even so, I made the change gladly; for I was sure of getting enough to eat, which is no small consideration to a hungry man.

The farmer and notorious slave-breaker to whom Thomas Auld hires Douglass.

## modern-en · caroline · 10:3

Mr. Covey's real talent was for deception. His whole life was given over to planning and pulling off the crudest lies. Whatever learning or religion he had, he bent to fit his urge to deceive. He seemed to believe he could even deceive God Almighty. He would say a short prayer in the morning and a long one at night, and, strange as it sounds, few men could at times look more devout than he did. The family worship always opened with singing, and since he was a wretched singer himself, the job of starting the hymn usually fell to me. He would read out the hymn and nod for me to begin. Sometimes I would; other times I would not. My refusal almost always threw everything into confusion. To show he did not need me, he would launch in and lurch through the hymn in the most off-key way imaginable. In that frame of mind he prayed with even more than his usual fervor. Poor man — such was his nature, and his skill at deceiving, that I honestly believe he sometimes fooled himself into the solemn conviction that he was a sincere worshipper of the Most High God; and this at a time when he could be said to be forcing his woman slave into the sin of adultery. Here are the facts. Mr. Covey was a poor man just starting out in life, able to afford only one slave, and — shocking as it is — he bought her, by his own account, as a breeder. The woman's name was Caroline. He bought her from Mr. Thomas Lowe, who lived about six miles from St. Michael's. She was large and strong, around twenty years old, and had already borne one child, which proved her to be exactly what he was after. After buying her, he hired a married man from Mr. Samuel Harrison to live with him for a year, and every night he locked the man up with her. The result was that at the end of the year the poor woman gave birth to twins. This outcome seemed to delight Mr. Covey, both with the man and with the wretched woman. So great was the joy of him and his wife that nothing they could do for Caroline during her confinement was too good or too much trouble. The children were counted as a fine addition to his wealth.

The enslaved woman purchased by Edward Covey.

## modern-en · smith · 10:9

I have already hinted that my situation was far worse during the first six months at Mr. Covey's than in the last six. What brought about the change in how he treated me marks a turning point in my humble story. You have seen how a man was made a slave; now you will see how a slave was made a man. On one of the hottest days of August 1833, Bill Smith, William Hughes, a slave named Eli, and I were busy fanning wheat. Hughes was clearing away the fanned wheat from the front of the fan, Eli was turning, Smith was feeding, and I was carrying the wheat over to the fan. The work was simple, calling for muscle rather than brains, but to someone wholly unused to it, it was punishing. Around three o'clock that afternoon I gave out — my strength failed, my head began pounding violently, I was swept with terrible dizziness, and every limb shook. Feeling it coming on, I braced myself, sure that stopping was out of the question. I held out as long as I could still stagger to the hopper with the grain. When I could no longer stay on my feet, I dropped, feeling as though some enormous weight were pinning me down. The fan of course came to a halt, since everyone had his own task and no one could do another man's work and keep his own going at the same time.

One of the men working alongside Douglass on Edward Covey’s farm.

## modern-en · eli · 10:9

I have already hinted that my situation was far worse during the first six months at Mr. Covey's than in the last six. What brought about the change in how he treated me marks a turning point in my humble story. You have seen how a man was made a slave; now you will see how a slave was made a man. On one of the hottest days of August 1833, Bill Smith, William Hughes, a slave named Eli, and I were busy fanning wheat. Hughes was clearing away the fanned wheat from the front of the fan, Eli was turning, Smith was feeding, and I was carrying the wheat over to the fan. The work was simple, calling for muscle rather than brains, but to someone wholly unused to it, it was punishing. Around three o'clock that afternoon I gave out — my strength failed, my head began pounding violently, I was swept with terrible dizziness, and every limb shook. Feeling it coming on, I braced myself, sure that stopping was out of the question. I held out as long as I could still stagger to the hopper with the grain. When I could no longer stay on my feet, I dropped, feeling as though some enormous weight were pinning me down. The fan of course came to a halt, since everyone had his own task and no one could do another man's work and keep his own going at the same time.

One of the men working alongside Douglass on Edward Covey’s farm.

## modern-en · kemp · 10:10

Mr. Covey was at the house, about a hundred yards from the treading-yard where we were fanning. The moment he heard the fan stop, he came straight over to where we were and hurriedly demanded to know what was wrong. Bill told him I was sick and there was no one left to bring wheat to the fan. By then I had crawled off under the post-and-rail fence that enclosed the yard, hoping to ease my misery by getting out of the sun. He asked where I was, and one of the hands told him. He came to the spot, looked at me a while, and asked what the matter was. I answered as best I could, for I barely had the strength to speak. At that he kicked me savagely in the side and ordered me to get up. I tried, but fell back. He kicked me again and told me again to rise. I tried once more and managed to get to my feet — but as I bent to grab the tub I had been feeding the fan with, I staggered and went down again. While I lay there, Mr. Covey picked up the hickory slat Hughes had been using to strike off the half-bushel measure and brought it down hard on my head, opening a large gash that bled freely; and with that he again told me to get up. I made no move to obey, having now made up my mind to let him do his worst. A short while after that blow, my head began to clear, and Mr. Covey had by then left me to my own devices. At that moment I decided, for the first time, to go to my master, lodge a complaint, and ask for his protection. To do it I would have to walk seven miles that afternoon, which under the circumstances was a brutal undertaking. I was terribly weak — as much from the kicks and blows as from the fierce attack of sickness I had been through. Still, I watched my chance, and while Covey was looking the other way, I set out for St. Michael's. I had gotten a fair distance on my way toward the woods when Covey spotted me and shouted for me to come back, threatening what he would do if I refused. I ignored both his calls and his threats and pushed on toward the woods as fast as my feeble state allowed; and thinking he might overtake me if I stayed on the road, I went through the woods, keeping far enough from the road to avoid being seen but close enough not to lose my way. I had not gone far before my little strength failed me again. I could go no farther. I fell down and lay there a long while, the blood still seeping from the wound on my head. For a time I thought I would bleed to death, and I believe I would have, had the blood not matted my hair enough to close the wound. After lying there about three-quarters of an hour, I steadied myself again and started on, through bogs and briars, barefoot and bareheaded, tearing my feet at nearly every step; and after a journey of about seven miles that took some five hours to make, I reached my master's store. The state I was in would have moved anyone but a heart of iron. From the top of my head to my feet I was covered in blood — my hair clotted with dust and blood, my shirt stiff with it. I must have looked like a man who had broken out of a den of wild beasts and barely gotten away. Looking like that, I stood before my master and humbly begged him to step in with his authority and protect me. I told him the whole story as well as I could, and as I spoke it seemed at times to move him. He would pace the floor and try to excuse Covey by saying he supposed I must have deserved it. He asked what I wanted. I told him I wanted a new home, because as sure as I lived with Mr. Covey again I would only live with him to die with him; Covey would surely kill me, and was well on the way to it. Master Thomas laughed off the notion that Covey was any danger to my life, said he knew the man, that he was a good man, and that he could not think of taking me away from him — that if he did, he would lose the whole year's wages, that I belonged to Covey for a year and had to go back to him no matter what, and that I had better not bother him with any more such stories, or he would get hold of me himself. After threatening me like that, he gave me a huge dose of salts, told me I could stay in St. Michael's that night, since it was already quite late, but that I had to be off back to Mr. Covey's first thing in the morning — and that if I wasn't, he would get hold of me, which meant he would whip me. I stayed the night, and as ordered I set out for Covey's in the morning, Saturday morning, worn out in body and broken in spirit. I had no supper that night and no breakfast that morning. I reached Covey's around nine o'clock; and just as I was climbing over the fence that separated Mrs. Kemp's fields from ours, out ran Covey with his cowskin to give me another whipping. Before he could get to me, I made it into the cornfield, and since the corn was very high it gave me a place to hide. He looked furious and searched for me a long time. My behavior must have seemed completely senseless to him. Finally he gave up the hunt, figuring, I suppose, that I would have to come home eventually for something to eat, and he would waste no more effort looking. I spent most of that day in the woods, with only two choices before me — go home and be whipped to death, or stay in the woods and starve. That night I ran into Sandy Jenkins, a slave I knew a little. Sandy had a free wife who lived about four miles from Mr. Covey's, and since it was Saturday he was on his way to see her. I told him my situation, and he kindly invited me to go home with him. I went, and we talked the whole matter over while I asked his advice on what I should do. I found Sandy an old hand at giving counsel. He told me very solemnly that I had to go back to Covey, but that before I did, I should go with him to another part of the woods where there was a certain root which, if I carried a piece of it always on my right side, would make it impossible for Mr. Covey, or any other white man, to whip me. He said he had carried it for years, and ever since he had, he had never taken a blow and never expected to as long as he kept it. At first I dismissed the idea that simply carrying a root in my pocket could do any such thing, and I was reluctant to take it; but Sandy pressed the matter earnestly, telling me it could do no harm even if it did no good. To please him I finally took the root and carried it on my right side as he had directed. This was Sunday morning. I started straight for home, and as I came into the yard gate, out came Mr. Covey, on his way to meeting. He spoke to me very kindly, told me to drive the pigs out of a nearby lot, and went on toward church. Now, this strange behavior of Mr. Covey's really did start me wondering whether there was something to the root Sandy had given me; and had it been any day but Sunday, I could have put his manner down to nothing but the power of that root. As it was, I was half ready to believe the root was more than I had first taken it for. All went well until Monday morning, when the root's power was truly put to the test. Long before daylight I was called to go and rub down, curry, and feed the horses. I obeyed, and was glad to. But while I was at it, just as I was tossing down some blades from the loft, Mr. Covey came into the stable with a long rope; and just as I was halfway out of the loft, he grabbed my legs and was about to tie me. As soon as I realized what he was up to, I gave a sudden lunge, and as I did, with him still gripping my legs, I came sprawling onto the stable floor. Mr. Covey seemed to think now that he had me and could do as he liked; but at that instant — where the spirit came from I cannot say — I made up my mind to fight, and acting on that resolve I seized Covey hard by the throat and rose up as I did so. He held on to me and I to him. My resistance was so completely unexpected that Covey seemed thrown off balance. He shook like a leaf. That gave me confidence, and I held him in a tight grip, drawing blood wherever my fingertips dug into him. Mr. Covey soon called out to Hughes for help. Hughes came, and while Covey held me he tried to tie my right hand. As he was at it, I watched my chance and gave him a hard kick just under the ribs. The kick truly sickened Hughes, so that he left me in Mr. Covey's hands. It not only weakened Hughes but Covey too. When Covey saw Hughes doubled over in pain, his nerve gave way. He asked if I meant to keep resisting. I told him I did, whatever came of it — that he had used me like a brute for six months, and I was determined to be used that way no longer. At that he tried to drag me toward a stick lying just outside the stable door, meaning to knock me down. But just as he leaned over to grab it, I caught him by the collar with both hands and snatched him suddenly to the ground. By this time Bill had come up. Covey called on him to help. Bill asked what he could do. Covey said, "Take hold of him, take hold of him!" Bill answered that his master had hired him out to work, not to help whip me, and so he left Covey and me to settle the fight ourselves. We went at it for nearly two hours. At last Covey let me go, puffing and blowing furiously, saying that if I had not resisted he would not have whipped me half so hard. The truth was, he had not whipped me at all. I reckoned he came out far the worse in the bargain, for he had drawn no blood from me, while I had drawn his. In all the six months I spent with Mr. Covey afterward, he never laid the weight of a finger on me in anger. Now and then he would say he had no wish to get hold of me again. "No," I thought, "you needn't — because you'll come off worse than you did before."

The neighbor through whose fields Douglass passes on his way to St. Michael’s.

## modern-en · freeland · 10:18

On January 1, 1834, I left Mr. Covey and went to live with Mr. William Freeland, who lived about three miles from St. Michael's. I soon found Mr. Freeland a very different man from Mr. Covey. He was not rich, but he was what would be called an educated southern gentleman. Mr. Covey, as I have shown, was a well-trained negro-breaker and slave-driver. Freeland, slaveholder though he was, seemed to have some regard for honor, some reverence for justice, and some respect for humanity. Covey seemed utterly dead to any such feelings. Mr. Freeland had many of the faults common to slaveholders — he was very hot-tempered and irritable — but in fairness I must say he was remarkably free of the degrading vices Mr. Covey was forever steeped in. Freeland was open and frank, and we always knew where to find him. Covey was a most cunning deceiver, who could be understood only by those sharp enough to see through his carefully laid frauds. Another advantage I gained in my new master was that he made no claim to religion, no profession of it — and that, in my view, was truly a great advantage. I say without the least hesitation that the religion of the south is nothing but a cloak for the most horrid crimes — a justifier of the most appalling barbarity, a sanctifier of the most hateful frauds, and a dark shelter under which the darkest, foulest, grossest, most hellish deeds of slaveholders find their firmest protection. If I were ever dragged back into the chains of slavery, then next to that enslavement itself I would count belonging to a religious master the worst calamity that could fall on me. For of all the slaveholders I have ever met, religious ones are the worst — the meanest and basest, the most cruel and cowardly of the lot. It was my wretched fate not only to belong to a religious slaveholder but to live in a whole community of such religionists. Right near Mr. Freeland lived the Rev. Daniel Weeden, and in the same neighborhood lived the Rev. Rigby Hopkins, both members and ministers in the Reformed Methodist Church. Among others, Mr. Weeden owned a woman slave whose name I have forgotten. For weeks at a time this woman's back was kept literally raw by the lash of that merciless, religious wretch. He used to hire hands. His rule was that whether a slave behaved well or behaved badly, it was a master's duty to whip him now and then to remind him of his master's authority. Such was his theory, and such was his practice.

The farmer near St. Michael’s who hires Douglass after his year with Covey.
