## modern-en · tom-graham · 10:38

Within a few moments they managed to tie John. Then they turned to Henry, who had come back by now, and ordered him to cross his hands. “I won’t!” said Henry firmly, showing he was ready to face whatever came of refusing. “Won’t you?” said Tom Graham, the constable. “No, I won’t!” said Henry, even more forcefully. At that, two of the constables drew their gleaming pistols and swore by their Creator that they would make him cross his hands or kill him. Each one cocked his pistol and, with his finger on the trigger, walked up to Henry, telling him as he came that if he did not cross his hands they would blow his damned heart out. “Shoot me, shoot me!” said Henry; “you can’t kill me but once. Shoot, shoot — and be damned! _I won’t be tied!_” He said this in a tone of loud defiance; and at the same instant, with a motion as fast as lightning, he knocked the pistols out of both constables’ hands with a single sweep of his arm. The moment he did, everyone fell on him, and after beating him for a while they finally overpowered him and got him tied.

One of the constables who comes to arrest Douglass and his companions.

## modern-en · betsy-freeland · 10:39

During the struggle, somehow — I don’t know how — I managed to get my pass out and slip it into the fire without anyone seeing. Now we were all tied; and just as we were about to be taken off to the Easton jail, Betsy Freeland, William Freeland’s mother, came to the door with her hands full of biscuits and shared them out between Henry and John. Then she unloaded a speech on us, which ran roughly like this — turning to me, she said, “_You devil! You yellow devil!_ it was you that put it into the heads of Henry and John to run away. But for you, you long-legged mulatto devil! Henry nor John would never have thought of such a thing.” I said nothing in reply, and was hurried off at once toward St. Michael’s. Only a moment before the scuffle with Henry, Mr. Hamilton had pointed out that they ought to search for the protections he had heard Frederick had written for himself and the rest. But just as he was about to act on the idea, he was needed to help tie Henry, and the commotion of the struggle made them either forget it or decide it was too risky, under the circumstances, to carry out the search. So we had not yet been convicted of meaning to run away.

William Freeland’s mother.

## modern-en · joseph-graham · 10:40

When we were about halfway to St. Michael’s, while the constables who had us in their charge were looking ahead, Henry asked me what he should do with his pass. I told him to eat it along with his biscuit and admit to nothing; and we passed the word down the line, “_Own nothing;_” and “_Own nothing!_” we all agreed. Our trust in one another had not been shaken at all. We were resolved to win or lose together — just as much after disaster had struck us as before. We were ready now for anything. That morning we were to be dragged fifteen miles behind horses and then locked up in the Easton jail. When we reached St. Michael’s, we went through a kind of examination. Every one of us denied that we had ever meant to run away. We did this more to draw out the evidence against us than from any hope of escaping being sold; for, as I have said, we were resigned to that. The truth was, we cared very little where we ended up, so long as we went together. Our biggest worry was being separated. We dreaded that more than anything this side of death. We learned that the evidence against us rested on the testimony of one person; our master would not say who it was, but among ourselves we reached a unanimous conclusion about who the informant had to be. We were sent off to the jail at Easton. When we got there, we were handed over to the sheriff, Mr. Joseph Graham, who put us in jail. Henry, John, and I were placed together in one room — Charles and Henry Bailey in another. Their aim in splitting us up was to keep us from acting together.

The sheriff who runs the Easton jail.

## modern-en · watson · 10:47

I went straight home and told Master Hugh the story of how I had been wronged; and I am glad to say of him that, irreligious as he was, his conduct was heavenly compared with how his brother Thomas had behaved in a similar situation. He listened closely as I laid out the events that led up to the brutal attack, and he showed plainly how furious it made him. The heart of my once over-kind mistress melted into pity again. My swollen-shut eye and blood-covered face brought her to tears. She pulled up a chair beside me, washed the blood from my face, and, with a mother’s tenderness, bound up my head, laying a thin slice of fresh beef over the wounded eye. Seeing kindness once more from this, my once affectionate old mistress, was almost worth all my suffering. Master Hugh was greatly enraged. He let his feelings out by pouring curses on the heads of those who had done it. As soon as I had recovered a little from my bruises, he took me with him to Esquire Watson’s on Bond Street to see what could be done about it. Mr. Watson asked who had witnessed the assault. Master Hugh told him it had been done at midday in Mr. Gardner’s ship-yard, where a large crowd of men were at work. “As to that,” he said, “the deed was done, and there was no question who did it.” Mr. Watson’s answer was that he could do nothing in the case unless some white man came forward to testify. He could issue no warrant on my word alone. Even if I had been killed in front of a thousand colored people, their combined testimony would not have been enough to arrest a single one of the murderers. Master Hugh, for once, was forced to admit that this state of things was too bad. Naturally, it proved impossible to find any white man willing to volunteer his testimony for me and against the white young men. Even those who may have felt for me were not prepared to do it. It took a kind of courage they did not have; for at just that time, the smallest show of humanity toward a colored person was branded as abolitionism, and that label exposed anyone who bore it to terrible dangers. The rallying cries of the bloody-minded in that region and in those days were, “Damn the abolitionists!” and “Damn the niggers!” Nothing was done, and probably nothing would have been done even if I had been killed. Such was, and such still is, the state of things in the Christian city of Baltimore.

The Baltimore official Hugh Auld consults about the assault on Douglass.

## modern-en · price · 10:48

Master Hugh, finding he could get no justice, refused to let me go back to Mr. Gardner. He kept me with him, and his wife tended my wound until I was nursed back to health. He then put me into the ship-yard where he was foreman, working for Mr. Walter Price. There I was set to calking right away, and very soon picked up the knack of handling my mallet and irons. Within a year of leaving Mr. Gardner’s, I was able to command the highest wages paid to the most experienced calkers. By then I was worth something to my master. I was bringing him in six or seven dollars a week. Sometimes I brought him nine dollars a week — my wages came to a dollar and a half a day. Once I had learned how to calk, I went out and found my own work, struck my own bargains, and collected the money I earned. My road grew much smoother than before; my life was now much more comfortable. When I could find no calking work, I did nothing. During those idle stretches, the old longings about freedom would come creeping back over me. While I worked for Mr. Gardner, I was kept in such a constant whirl of commotion that I could think of almost nothing but staying alive; and in thinking of my life, I nearly forgot my liberty. I have noticed this throughout my time in slavery — that whenever my situation got better, it did not make me more content; it only sharpened my craving to be free and set me to plotting ways of gaining my freedom. I have found that, to make a slave contented, you must first make him thoughtless. You have to darken his moral and mental sight and, as far as you can, wipe out his power to reason. He must be unable to spot any contradiction in slavery; he must be made to feel that slavery is right; and he can only be brought to that point once he has stopped being a man.

The shipyard owner for whom Hugh Auld works as foreman.

## modern-en · butler · 11:3

About two months later I went to Master Hugh and asked for the privilege of hiring my time. He had no idea I had already asked Master Thomas and been turned down. He too seemed inclined to refuse at first, but after thinking it over he granted the privilege and set these terms: I could have all my own time, make my own contracts with whoever hired me, and find my own work; and in exchange for that freedom I had to pay him three dollars at the end of every week and supply my own calking tools, board, and clothing. My board cost two and a half dollars a week. That, plus the wear and tear on clothes and calking tools, brought my regular expenses to about six dollars a week. I had to come up with that amount or give up the privilege of hiring my time. Rain or shine, work or no work, the money had to appear at the end of each week, or I lost my privilege. As anyone can see, the arrangement strongly favored my master. It freed him from any need to keep an eye on me. His money was guaranteed. He got all the benefits of slaveholding with none of its troubles, while I bore all the troubles of a slave and carried all the worry and anxiety of a free man besides. I found it a hard bargain. But hard as it was, I thought it better than the old way of getting by. Being allowed to shoulder a free man's responsibilities was a step toward freedom, and I was determined to hang on to it. I threw myself into making money. I was willing to work nights as well as days, and through tireless effort and industry I earned enough to cover my expenses and put a little money aside every week. I went on like this from May until August. Then Master Hugh refused to let me hire my time any longer. His reason was that one Saturday night I had failed to pay him for my week's time. The failure happened because I went to a camp meeting about ten miles from Baltimore. During the week I had arranged with a group of young friends to leave Baltimore for the camp ground early Saturday evening, and since my employer kept me late, I could not get down to Master Hugh's without letting the company down. I knew Master Hugh had no special need of the money that night. So I decided to go to the camp meeting and pay him the three dollars when I got back. I stayed at the camp meeting one day longer than I had planned when I set out. But the moment I returned I went to him to pay what he reckoned he was owed. I found him furious; he could barely hold his anger in. He said he had half a mind to give me a hard whipping. He demanded to know how I dared leave the city without asking his permission. I told him I hired my time, and as long as I paid him the price he asked for it, I did not see that I was bound to ask him when and where I could go. That answer upset him, and after thinking it over for a few moments, he turned to me and said I would hire my time no longer — the next thing he knew, I would be running away. On that same excuse, he told me to bring my tools and clothing home at once. I did, but instead of looking for work, as I used to do before I hired my time, I spent the entire week without doing a single stroke of work. I did it out of spite. Saturday night he came to me as usual for my week's wages. I told him I had no wages; I had done no work that week. At that point we nearly came to blows. He raved and swore he would get hold of me. I did not say a single word, but I was resolved that if he laid a hand on me it would be blow for blow. He did not strike me, but said that from then on he would keep me in steady work himself. I thought it all over the next day, Sunday, and finally settled on the third of September as the day I would make a second try at securing my freedom. That gave me three weeks to prepare for the journey. Early Monday morning, before Master Hugh had a chance to line up any work for me, I went out and got a job from Mr. Butler at his ship-yard near the drawbridge, on what is called the City Block — which made it unnecessary for him to find work for me. At the end of the week I brought him between eight and nine dollars. He seemed very pleased and asked why I had not done the same the week before. He had no idea what my plans were. My point in working steadily was to clear away any suspicion he might have that I meant to run, and I succeeded perfectly. I suppose he believed I had never been more content with my situation than during the very weeks I was plotting my escape. The second week went by, and again I handed him my full wages; he was so pleased that he gave me twenty-five cents — a fairly large sum for a slaveholder to give a slave — and told me to put it to good use. I said I would.

The Baltimore shipyard owner from whom Douglass obtains work.

## modern-en · ruggles · 11:6

Thankfully, I did not stay trapped in that misery for long. The kindness of _Mr. David Ruggles_ rescued me from it — a man whose watchfulness, kindness, and persistence I will never forget. I am glad of the chance to express, as far as words can, the love and gratitude I feel toward him. Mr. Ruggles is now stricken with blindness, and is himself in need of the very kindnesses he was once so eager to perform for others. I had been in New York only a few days when Mr. Ruggles sought me out and very kindly took me to his boarding-house at the corner of Church and Lespenard Streets. At the time Mr. Ruggles was deeply caught up in the famous _Darg_ case, while also looking after a number of other fugitive slaves and working out ways and means for their escape; and though he was watched and hemmed in on nearly every side, he seemed more than a match for his enemies.

The New York abolitionist who assists people escaping slavery.

## modern-en · anna · 11:7

Very soon after I came to Mr. Ruggles, he wanted to know where I wished to go, since he thought it unsafe for me to stay in New York. I told him I was a calker and would like to go where I could find work. I considered going to Canada, but he decided against it and in favor of New Bedford, believing I could get work there at my trade. Around this time Anna,[2] my intended wife, arrived; for right after I reached New York I had written to her — despite my homeless, houseless, and helpless condition — telling her of my successful escape and asking her to come on at once. Pennington, who, in the presence of Mr. Ruggles, Mrs. Michaels, and two or three others, performed the marriage ceremony and gave us a certificate, an exact copy of which follows:—

## modern-en · shaw · 11:12

Once I had this certificate and a five-dollar bill from Mr. Ruggles, I hoisted one part of our baggage, Anna took up the other, and we set off at once to board the steamboat John W. Richmond for Newport, on our way to New Bedford. Mr. Ruggles gave me a letter for a Mr. Shaw in Newport and told me that if my money did not stretch as far as New Bedford, I should stop in Newport and get more help. But when we reached Newport we were so eager to get somewhere safe that, even though we lacked the money for our fare, we decided to take seats in the stage and promise to pay once we got to New Bedford. Two fine gentlemen, residents of New Bedford, encouraged us to do this; I later learned their names were Joseph Ricketson and William C. Taber. They seemed to understand our circumstances at once and gave us such assurances of their goodwill that we felt completely at ease around them.

The Newport contact to whom David Ruggles directs Douglass for help.

## modern-en · nathan · 11:13

It was good indeed to meet such friends at such a time. When we reached New Bedford, we were sent to the house of Mr. Nathan Johnson, who received us kindly and gave us a hospitable welcome. Both Mr. and Mrs. Johnson took a deep, warm interest in our well-being. They proved themselves fully worthy of the name abolitionist. When the stage-driver found we could not pay our fare, he kept hold of our baggage as security for the debt. I had only to mention this to Mr. Johnson, and he advanced the money right away.

## modern-en · coffin · 11:21

I had not been reading the “Liberator” long before I had a fairly clear grasp of the principles, methods, and spirit of the anti-slavery reform. I threw myself into the cause. I could do only a little, but what I could do I did with a joyful heart, and I never felt happier than when I was at an anti-slavery meeting. I rarely had much to say at these meetings, because whatever I wanted to say was said far better by others. But while I was attending an anti-slavery convention at Nantucket, on the 11th of August, 1841, I felt strongly moved to speak, and at the same time I was urged to do so by Mr. William C. Coffin, a gentleman who had heard me speak at the colored people's meeting in New Bedford. It was a heavy burden, and I took it up reluctantly. The truth was, I still felt myself a slave, and the idea of speaking to white people weighed me down. I had spoken only a few moments when I felt a measure of freedom and said what I wanted with considerable ease. From that time until now I have been engaged in pleading the cause of my brethren — with what success, and with what devotion, I leave to those familiar with my work to decide.

The abolitionist who encourages Douglass to address the Nantucket convention.

## modern-en · ham · 1:6

Every year brings multitudes of this class of slaves. It was surely an awareness of this fact that led one great Southern statesman to predict that slavery would fall by the inevitable laws of population. Whether or not that prophecy is ever fulfilled, it is plain that a very different-looking class of people is now growing up in the South and being held in slavery—people quite unlike those first brought to this country from Africa; and if their increase does no other good, it will at least undo the force of the argument that God cursed Ham, and that American slavery is therefore right. If only the direct descendants of Ham may scripturally be enslaved, then slavery in the South must soon become unscriptural, for thousands are born into the world every year who, like me, owe their existence to white fathers—and most often to fathers who are also their masters.

## modern-en · job · 3:3

To describe Colonel Lloyd's wealth would be almost like describing the riches of Job. He kept between ten and fifteen house-servants. He was said to own a thousand slaves, and I think that figure is well within the truth. He owned so many that he did not recognize them when he saw them, and not all the slaves on the out-farms knew him by sight either. The story goes that one day, riding along the road, he met a colored man and spoke to him in the way white men usually addressed colored people on the public roads of the South: “Well, boy, whom do you belong to?” “To Colonel Lloyd,” the slave replied. “Well, does the colonel treat you well?” “No, sir,” came the ready answer. “What, does he work you too hard?” “Yes, sir.” “Well, doesn't he give you enough to eat?” “Yes, sir, he gives me enough, such as it is.”

The biblical figure whose wealth supplies a comparison for Colonel Lloyd’s possessions.

## modern-en · sheridan · 7:5

In the same book, I came upon one of Sheridan’s mighty speeches on behalf of Catholic emancipation. These were precious documents to me. I read them over and over again with undiminished interest. They gave voice to interesting thoughts of my own soul, which had often flashed through my mind only to fade away for lack of expression. The lesson I drew from the dialogue was the power of truth over the conscience of even a slaveholder. What I got from Sheridan was a bold denunciation of slavery and a powerful defense of human rights. Reading these documents enabled me to put my thoughts into words and to answer the arguments brought forward to support slavery; but while they relieved me of one difficulty, they brought on another even more painful than the one they had relieved. The more I read, the more I came to abhor and detest my enslavers. I could see them in no other light than as a band of successful robbers who had left their homes, gone to Africa, stolen us from our homes, and reduced us to slavery in a strange land. I loathed them as the meanest and most wicked of men. As I read and reflected on the subject, behold!—that very discontent which Master Hugh had predicted would follow my learning to read had already arrived, to torment and sting my soul into unspeakable anguish. As I writhed under it, I would at times feel that learning to read had been a curse rather than a blessing. It had shown me my wretched condition without offering any remedy. It had opened my eyes to the horrible pit, but gave me no ladder to climb out of it. In moments of agony, I envied my fellow-slaves their dullness. I have often wished myself a beast. I would have preferred the condition of the lowest reptile to my own. Anything, no matter what, to be rid of thinking! It was this endless thinking about my condition that tormented me. There was no escaping it. It was forced upon me by every object within sight or hearing, living or lifeless. The silver trumpet of freedom had roused my soul to permanent wakefulness. Freedom now appeared, never to vanish again. It was heard in every sound and seen in everything. It was always there to torment me with a sense of my wretched condition. I saw nothing without seeing it, I heard nothing without hearing it, and felt nothing without feeling it. It looked out from every star, it smiled in every calm, breathed in every wind, and moved in every storm.

The Irish-born orator whose speech on Catholic emancipation Douglass reads.

## modern-en · webster · 7:7

The idea of how I might learn to write came to me from being in Durgin and Bailey’s ship-yard, where I often saw the ship carpenters, after hewing a piece of timber and getting it ready for use, write on it the name of the part of the ship it was meant for. When a piece of timber was meant for the larboard side, it would be marked like this—“L.” When a piece was for the starboard side, it would be marked like this—“S.” A piece for the larboard side forward would be marked like this—“L. F.” When a piece was for the starboard side forward, it would be marked like this—“S. F.” For larboard aft, it would be marked like this—“L. A.” For starboard aft, it would be marked like this—“S. A.” I soon learned the names of these letters and what they stood for when placed on a piece of timber in the ship-yard. I immediately began copying them, and in a short time I was able to make the four letters named. After that, whenever I met a boy I knew could write, I would tell him I could write as well as he could. His next words would be, “I don’t believe you. Let me see you try it.” I would then make the letters I had been lucky enough to learn, and ask him to beat that. In this way I got a good many lessons in writing, which I quite possibly would never have gotten in any other way. During this time, my copy-book was the board fence, the brick wall, and the pavement; my pen and ink was a lump of chalk. With these, I learned mostly how to write. I then began copying the italics in Webster’s Spelling Book, and kept at it until I could make them all without looking at the book. By this time, my little Master Thomas had gone to school, learned how to write, and filled up a number of copy-books. These had been brought home, shown to some of our near neighbors, and then set aside. My mistress used to go to class meeting at the Wilk Street meetinghouse every Monday afternoon and leave me to look after the house. When left like this, I would spend the time writing in the spaces left in Master Thomas’s copy-book, copying what he had written. I kept doing this until I could write a hand very much like Master Thomas’s. And so, after a long, tedious effort over many years, I finally succeeded in learning how to write.

The author of the spelling book Douglass uses while learning to write.

## modern-en · whittier · 8:5

Very soon after my return to Baltimore, my mistress, Lucretia, died, leaving behind her husband and one child, Amanda; and a very short time after her death, Master Andrew died as well. Now all the property of my old master, slaves included, was in the hands of strangers—strangers who had had nothing to do with building it up. Not a single slave was set free. All remained slaves, from the youngest to the oldest. If any one thing in my experience, more than any other, served to deepen my conviction of the hellish character of slavery, and to fill me with unspeakable loathing of slaveholders, it was their base ingratitude toward my poor old grandmother. She had served my old master faithfully from her youth into old age. She had been the source of all his wealth; she had peopled his plantation with slaves; she had become a great-grandmother in his service. She had rocked him in infancy, attended him in childhood, served him throughout his life, and at his death wiped the cold death-sweat from his icy brow and closed his eyes forever. Yet she was still left a slave—a slave for life—a slave in the hands of strangers; and in their hands she watched her children, her grandchildren, and her great-grandchildren divided up, like so many sheep, without being granted even the small privilege of a single word about their fate or her own. And to crown their base ingratitude and fiendish cruelty, my grandmother, who was now very old, having outlived my old master and all his children, having seen the beginning and the end of every one of them—when her present owners found that she was of little value, her frame already racked with the pains of old age and complete helplessness rapidly overtaking her once active limbs, they carried her off to the woods, built her a little hut, put up a small mud chimney, and then graciously left her to support herself there in utter loneliness; thus, in effect, turning her out to die! If my poor old grandmother is still alive, she lives to suffer in total loneliness; she lives to remember and mourn the loss of her children, the loss of her grandchildren, and the loss of her great-grandchildren. They are, in the words of the slave’s poet, Whittier,—

The antislavery poet quoted in Douglass’s account of his grandmother.

## modern-en · napoleon · 9:1

As bad as all slaveholders are, we rarely meet one who lacks every element of character that commands respect. My master was one of this rare sort. I do not know of a single noble act he ever performed. The leading trait in his character was meanness, and if there was any other quality in his nature, it was made to serve this one. He was mean, and, like most other mean men, he lacked the ability to hide his meanness. Captain Auld was not born a slaveholder. He had been a poor man, master only of a Bay craft. He came into possession of all his slaves through marriage; and of all men, those who take up slaveholding by adoption are the worst. He was cruel, but cowardly. He gave commands without firmness. In enforcing his rules he was sometimes rigid and sometimes lax. At times he spoke to his slaves with the firmness of Napoleon and the fury of a demon; at other times he might easily have been mistaken for a lost traveler asking directions. He did nothing on his own. He might have passed for a lion, were it not for his ears. In every noble thing he attempted, his own meanness showed through most plainly. His airs, his words, and his actions were the airs, words, and actions of born slaveholders, and, being merely put on, they were awkward enough. He was not even a good imitator. He had every inclination to deceive but not the power. Having no resources within himself, he was forced to copy many others, and being a mere copyist, he was forever the victim of inconsistency; and as a result, he was an object of contempt, even in the eyes of his own slaves. The luxury of having slaves of his own to wait on him was something new to him and something he was unprepared for. He was a slaveholder without the ability to hold slaves. He found himself incapable of managing his slaves by force, by fear, or by fraud. We seldom called him “master;” we generally called him “Captain Auld,” and were hardly inclined to give him any title at all. I have no doubt that our conduct had a great deal to do with making him seem awkward, and therefore irritable. Our lack of reverence for him must have puzzled him greatly. He wanted us to call him master but lacked the firmness needed to command us to do so. His wife used to insist that we call him so, but to no effect. In August, 1832, my master attended a Methodist camp-meeting held in the Bay-side, Talbot county, and there he experienced religion. I held a faint hope that his conversion would lead him to free his slaves, and that, if it did not do that, it would at least make him kinder and more humane. I was disappointed on both counts. It neither made him humane toward his slaves nor moved him to free them. If it had any effect on his character, it made him more cruel and hateful in everything he did; for I believe he was a far worse man after his conversion than before. Before his conversion, he relied on his own depravity to shield and sustain him in his savage barbarity; but after his conversion, he found religious sanction and support for his slaveholding cruelty. He made the loudest claims to piety. His house was a house of prayer. He prayed morning, noon, and night. He very soon distinguished himself among his brethren and was quickly made a class-leader and exhorter. He was very active in revivals, and he proved himself a useful instrument in the church’s hands for converting many souls. His house was a home for the preachers. They used to take great pleasure in coming there to stay; for while he starved us, he stuffed them. We have had three or four preachers there at once. The names of those who came most often while I lived there were Mr. Storks, Mr. Ewery, Mr. Humphry, and Mr. Hickey. I have also seen Mr. George Cookman at our house. We slaves loved Mr. Cookman. We believed him to be a good man. We thought he had been instrumental in getting Mr. Samuel Harrison, a very rich slaveholder, to free his slaves; and somehow we got the impression that he was working to bring about the emancipation of all slaves. Whenever he was at our house, we were sure to be called in to prayers. When the others were there, we were sometimes called in and sometimes not. Mr. Cookman took more notice of us than any of the other ministers. He could not be among us without showing his sympathy for us, and, ignorant as we were, we had the sense to see it.

The French military ruler invoked in a comparison with Thomas Auld’s manner.

When we finally settled on the firm decision to run away, we risked more than Patrick Henry did when he chose liberty or death. For us, liberty was doubtful at best, and death almost certain if we failed. As for me, I would take death over hopeless bondage.

The American revolutionary orator quoted on liberty and death.

## modern-en · god · 1:6

Every year brings multitudes of this class of slaves. It was surely an awareness of this fact that led one great Southern statesman to predict that slavery would fall by the inevitable laws of population. Whether or not that prophecy is ever fulfilled, it is plain that a very different-looking class of people is now growing up in the South and being held in slavery—people quite unlike those first brought to this country from Africa; and if their increase does no other good, it will at least undo the force of the argument that God cursed Ham, and that American slavery is therefore right. ## modern-en · jesus · 10:23

Henry and John were quite intelligent, and very soon after I came I managed to stir up in them a strong wish to learn to read. That wish soon caught on with the others too. They quickly rounded up a few old spelling-books, and nothing would do but that I should hold a Sabbath school. I agreed, and so I gave up my Sundays to teaching these dear fellow-slaves to read. Neither of them knew his letters when I arrived. Some of the slaves from the neighboring farms heard what was going on and took advantage of this little chance to learn to read as well. It was understood among everyone who came that there must be as little show about it as possible. We had to keep our religious masters at St. Michael's from finding out that, instead of spending the Sabbath wrestling, boxing, and drinking whisky, we were trying to learn to read the will of God; for they would far rather see us busy with those degrading sports than see us acting like intelligent, moral, accountable human beings. My blood boils when I think of the bloody way that Messrs. Wright Fairbanks and Garrison West, both class-leaders, came rushing in on us along with many others, armed with sticks and stones, and broke up our innocent little Sabbath school at St. Michael's — all of them calling themselves Christians, humble followers of the Lord Jesus Christ! But here I am wandering off the subject again.

The central figure of Christianity, whose teachings Douglass contrasts with slaveholding religion.

## modern-en · father · 1:2

My father was a white man. Everyone I ever heard speak of my parentage took that for granted. It was also whispered that my master was my father, but I know nothing about whether that was true; I was kept from any means of finding out. My mother and I were separated while I was still an infant, before I ever knew her as my mother. In the part of Maryland I escaped from, it was common practice to separate children from their mothers very early. Often, before a child had even reached its first year, the mother would be taken away and hired out to a farm some distance off, and the child left in the care of an old woman too old for field work. I do not know why this was done, unless it was to keep the child from forming any attachment to its mother, and to dull and destroy the mother's natural love for her child. That is the unavoidable result.

The white man who fathered Douglass; the narrator does not know his identity with certainty.

## modern-en · brother · 8:3

I suffered more anxiety than most of my fellow slaves. I had known what it was to be treated kindly; they had known nothing of the sort. They had seen little or nothing of the world. They were truly men and women of sorrow, well acquainted with grief. Their backs had grown familiar with the bloody lash, until they had become hardened to it; mine was still tender, for while in Baltimore I had received few whippings, and few slaves could claim a kinder master and mistress than I had. The thought of passing out of their hands into those of Master Andrew—a man who, only a few days before, had given me a sample of his bloody temper by seizing my little brother by the throat, throwing him to the ground, and stamping on his head with the heel of his boot until the blood gushed from his nose and ears—was well suited to make me anxious about what lay ahead. After committing this savage outrage upon my brother, he turned to me and said that was exactly how he intended to treat me one of these days—meaning, I suppose, once I came into his possession.

Douglass’s younger brother, enslaved on the Anthony estate.

## modern-en · cousin-anna · 4:6

The wife of Mr. Giles Hicks, who lived only a short distance from where I used to live, murdered my wife’s cousin, a young girl between fifteen and sixteen years of age, mangling her body in the most horrible way, breaking her nose and her breastbone with a stick, so that the poor girl died a few hours later. She was buried at once, but had been in her untimely grave only a few hours before she was dug up and examined by the coroner, who ruled that she had died from a severe beating. The offense for which this girl was murdered was this: she had been put in charge that night of minding Mrs. Hicks’s baby, and during the night she fell asleep, and the baby cried. Having lost her sleep for several nights before, she did not hear the crying. They were both in the room with Mrs. Hicks. Mrs. Hicks, finding the girl slow to get up, leapt from her bed, snatched an oak stick of wood from the fireplace, and with it broke the girl’s nose and breastbone, and so ended her life. I will not say that this most horrible murder produced no stir in the community. It did produce a stir, but not enough to bring the murderess to punishment. A warrant was issued for her arrest, but it was never carried out. So she escaped not only punishment, but even the discomfort of being brought before a court for her horrible crime.

Douglass’s wife’s young cousin, enslaved in the Hicks household.

## modern-en · sandy-wife · 10:10

Mr. Covey was at the house, about a hundred yards from the treading-yard where we were fanning. The moment he heard the fan stop, he came straight over to where we were and hurriedly demanded to know what was wrong. Bill told him I was sick and there was no one left to bring wheat to the fan. By then I had crawled off under the post-and-rail fence that enclosed the yard, hoping to ease my misery by getting out of the sun. He asked where I was, and one of the hands told him. He came to the spot, looked at me a while, and asked what the matter was. I answered as best I could, for I barely had the strength to speak. At that he kicked me savagely in the side and ordered me to get up. I tried, but fell back. He kicked me again and told me again to rise. I tried once more and managed to get to my feet — but as I bent to grab the tub I had been feeding the fan with, I staggered and went down again. While I lay there, Mr. Covey picked up the hickory slat Hughes had been using to strike off the half-bushel measure and brought it down hard on my head, opening a large gash that bled freely; and with that he again told me to get up. I made no move to obey, having now made up my mind to let him do his worst. A short while after that blow, my head began to clear, and Mr. Covey had by then left me to my own devices. At that moment I decided, for the first time, to go to my master, lodge a complaint, and ask for his protection. To do it I would have to walk seven miles that afternoon, which under the circumstances was a brutal undertaking. I was terribly weak — as much from the kicks and blows as from the fierce attack of sickness I had been through. Still, I watched my chance, and while Covey was looking the other way, I set out for St. Michael's. I had gotten a fair distance on my way toward the woods when Covey spotted me and shouted for me to come back, threatening what he would do if I refused. I ignored both his calls and his threats and pushed on toward the woods as fast as my feeble state allowed; and thinking he might overtake me if I stayed on the road, I went through the woods, keeping far enough from the road to avoid being seen but close enough not to lose my way. I had not gone far before my little strength failed me again. I could go no farther. I fell down and lay there a long while, the blood still seeping from the wound on my head. For a time I thought I would bleed to death, and I believe I would have, had the blood not matted my hair enough to close the wound. After lying there about three-quarters of an hour, I steadied myself again and started on, through bogs and briars, barefoot and bareheaded, tearing my feet at nearly every step; and after a journey of about seven miles that took some five hours to make, I reached my master's store. The state I was in would have moved anyone but a heart of iron. From the top of my head to my feet I was covered in blood — my hair clotted with dust and blood, my shirt stiff with it. I must have looked like a man who had broken out of a den of wild beasts and barely gotten away. Looking like that, I stood before my master and humbly begged him to step in with his authority and protect me. I told him the whole story as well as I could, and as I spoke it seemed at times to move him. He would pace the floor and try to excuse Covey by saying he supposed I must have deserved it. He asked what I wanted. I told him I wanted a new home, because as sure as I lived with Mr. Covey again I would only live with him to die with him; Covey would surely kill me, and was well on the way to it. Master Thomas laughed off the notion that Covey was any danger to my life, said he knew the man, that he was a good man, and that he could not think of taking me away from him — that if he did, he would lose the whole year's wages, that I belonged to Covey for a year and had to go back to him no matter what, and that I had better not bother him with any more such stories, or he would get hold of me himself. After threatening me like that, he gave me a huge dose of salts, told me I could stay in St. Michael's that night, since it was already quite late, but that I had to be off back to Mr. Covey's first thing in the morning — and that if I wasn't, he would get hold of me, which meant he would whip me. I stayed the night, and as ordered I set out for Covey's in the morning, Saturday morning, worn out in body and broken in spirit. I had no supper that night and no breakfast that morning. I reached Covey's around nine o'clock; and just as I was climbing over the fence that separated Mrs. Kemp's fields from ours, out ran Covey with his cowskin to give me another whipping. Before he could get to me, I made it into the cornfield, and since the corn was very high it gave me a place to hide. He looked furious and searched for me a long time. My behavior must have seemed completely senseless to him. Finally he gave up the hunt, figuring, I suppose, that I would have to come home eventually for something to eat, and he would waste no more effort looking. I spent most of that day in the woods, with only two choices before me — go home and be whipped to death, or stay in the woods and starve. That night I ran into Sandy Jenkins, a slave I knew a little. Sandy had a free wife who lived about four miles from Mr. Covey's, and since it was Saturday he was on his way to see her. I told him my situation, and he kindly invited me to go home with him. I went, and we talked the whole matter over while I asked his advice on what I should do. I found Sandy an old hand at giving counsel. He told me very solemnly that I had to go back to Covey, but that before I did, I should go with him to another part of the woods where there was a certain root which, if I carried a piece of it always on my right side, would make it impossible for Mr. Covey, or any other white man, to whip me. He said he had carried it for years, and ever since he had, he had never taken a blow and never expected to as long as he kept it. At first I dismissed the idea that simply carrying a root in my pocket could do any such thing, and I was reluctant to take it; but Sandy pressed the matter earnestly, telling me it could do no harm even if it did no good. To please him I finally took the root and carried it on my right side as he had directed. This was Sunday morning. I started straight for home, and as I came into the yard gate, out came Mr. Covey, on his way to meeting. He spoke to me very kindly, told me to drive the pigs out of a nearby lot, and went on toward church. Now, this strange behavior of Mr. Covey's really did start me wondering whether there was something to the root Sandy had given me; and had it been any day but Sunday, I could have put his manner down to nothing but the power of that root. As it was, I was half ready to believe the root was more than I had first taken it for. All went well until Monday morning, when the root's power was truly put to the test. Long before daylight I was called to go and rub down, curry, and feed the horses. I obeyed, and was glad to. But while I was at it, just as I was tossing down some blades from the loft, Mr. Covey came into the stable with a long rope; and just as I was halfway out of the loft, he grabbed my legs and was about to tie me. As soon as I realized what he was up to, I gave a sudden lunge, and as I did, with him still gripping my legs, I came sprawling onto the stable floor. Mr. Covey seemed to think now that he had me and could do as he liked; but at that instant — where the spirit came from I cannot say — I made up my mind to fight, and acting on that resolve I seized Covey hard by the throat and rose up as I did so. He held on to me and I to him. My resistance was so completely unexpected that Covey seemed thrown off balance. He shook like a leaf. That gave me confidence, and I held him in a tight grip, drawing blood wherever my fingertips dug into him. Mr. Covey soon called out to Hughes for help. Hughes came, and while Covey held me he tried to tie my right hand. As he was at it, I watched my chance and gave him a hard kick just under the ribs. The kick truly sickened Hughes, so that he left me in Mr. Covey's hands. It not only weakened Hughes but Covey too. When Covey saw Hughes doubled over in pain, his nerve gave way. He asked if I meant to keep resisting. I told him I did, whatever came of it — that he had used me like a brute for six months, and I was determined to be used that way no longer. At that he tried to drag me toward a stick lying just outside the stable door, meaning to knock me down. But just as he leaned over to grab it, I caught him by the collar with both hands and snatched him suddenly to the ground. By this time Bill had come up. Covey called on him to help. Bill asked what he could do. Covey said, "Take hold of him, take hold of him!" Bill answered that his master had hired him out to work, not to help whip me, and so he left Covey and me to settle the fight ourselves. We went at it for nearly two hours. At last Covey let me go, puffing and blowing furiously, saying that if I had not resisted he would not have whipped me half so hard. The truth was, he had not whipped me at all. I reckoned he came out far the worse in the bargain, for he had drawn no blood from me, while I had drawn his. In all the six months I spent with Mr. Covey afterward, he never laid the weight of a finger on me in anger. Now and then he would say he had no wish to get hold of me again. "No," I thought, "you needn't — because you'll come off worse than you did before."

Sandy Jenkins’s wife, a free woman living near Covey’s farm.

## modern-en · mrs-covey · 10:3

Mr. Covey's real talent was for deception. His whole life was given over to planning and pulling off the crudest lies. Whatever learning or religion he had, he bent to fit his urge to deceive. He seemed to believe he could even deceive God Almighty. He would say a short prayer in the morning and a long one at night, and, strange as it sounds, few men could at times look more devout than he did. The family worship always opened with singing, and since he was a wretched singer himself, the job of starting the hymn usually fell to me. He would read out the hymn and nod for me to begin. Sometimes I would; other times I would not. My refusal almost always threw everything into confusion. To show he did not need me, he would launch in and lurch through the hymn in the most off-key way imaginable. In that frame of mind he prayed with even more than his usual fervor. Poor man — such was his nature, and his skill at deceiving, that I honestly believe he sometimes fooled himself into the solemn conviction that he was a sincere worshipper of the Most High God; and this at a time when he could be said to be forcing his woman slave into the sin of adultery. Here are the facts. Mr. Covey was a poor man just starting out in life, able to afford only one slave, and — shocking as it is — he bought her, by his own account, as a breeder. The woman's name was Caroline. He bought her from Mr. Thomas Lowe, who lived about six miles from St. Michael's. She was large and strong, around twenty years old, and had already borne one child, which proved her to be exactly what he was after. After buying her, he hired a married man from Mr. Samuel Harrison to live with him for a year, and every night he locked the man up with her. The result was that at the end of the year the poor woman gave birth to twins. This outcome seemed to delight Mr. Covey, both with the man and with the wretched woman. So great was the joy of him and his wife that nothing they could do for Caroline during her confinement was too good or too much trouble. The children were counted as a fine addition to his wealth.

Edward Covey’s wife.

## modern-en · irishmen · 7:6

I often found myself regretting my own existence and wishing myself dead; and were it not for the hope of being free, I have no doubt that I would have killed myself, or done something for which I would have been killed. While in this state of mind, I was eager to hear anyone speak of slavery. I was a ready listener. Every now and then, I would hear something about the abolitionists. It was some time before I discovered what the word meant. It was always used in such contexts as to make it an intriguing word to me. If a slave ran away and managed to get clear, or if a slave killed his master, set fire to a barn, or did anything that struck a slaveholder as very wrong, it was spoken of as the fruit of _abolition._ Hearing the word in this connection so often, I set out to learn what it meant. The dictionary gave me little or no help. I found that it meant “the act of abolishing;” but then I did not know what was to be abolished. Here I was baffled. I did not dare ask anyone about its meaning, for I was sure it was something they wanted me to know very little about. After waiting patiently, I got hold of one of our city papers, which contained a report of the number of petitions from the north praying for the abolition of slavery in the District of Columbia and of the slave trade between the States. From this time on I understood the words _abolition_ and _abolitionist,_ and I always drew near whenever that word was spoken, expecting to hear something of importance to myself and my fellow-slaves. The light broke in on me little by little. One day I went down to the wharf of Mr. Waters; and seeing two Irishmen unloading a scow of stone, I went over, unasked, and helped them. When we had finished, one of them came up to me and asked if I were a slave. I told him I was. He asked, “Are ye a slave for life?” I told him that I was. The good Irishman seemed deeply moved by my answer. He said to the other that it was a pity so fine a little fellow as myself should be a slave for life. He said it was a shame to hold me. They both advised me to run away to the north; there I would find friends, and there I would be free. I pretended not to be interested in what they said, and acted as if I did not understand them; for I feared they might be treacherous. White men have been known to encourage slaves to escape, and then, to claim the reward, catch them and return them to their masters. I was afraid these seemingly good men might do the same to me; but all the same I remembered their advice, and from that time on I resolved to run away. I looked forward to a time when it would be safe for me to escape. I was too young to think of doing it immediately; besides, I wanted to learn how to write, since I might have need to write my own pass. I comforted myself with the hope that one day I would find a good chance. In the meantime, I would learn to write.

The men Douglass helps unload a scow at Mr. Waters’s wharf.

## modern-en · schoolboys · 7:3

The plan I adopted, and the one that worked best for me, was to make friends of all the little white boys I met in the street. As many of these as I could, I turned into teachers. With their kindly help, given at different times and in different places, I finally managed to learn to read. Whenever I was sent on errands, I always took my book with me, and by hurrying through one part of an errand, I found time to get a lesson before I returned. I also used to carry bread with me—there was always plenty of it in the house, and I was always welcome to it; for in this respect I was much better off than many of the poor white children in our neighborhood. This bread I gave away to the hungry little urchins, who in return would give me that far more valuable bread of knowledge. I am strongly tempted to give the names of two or three of those little boys, as a testimony to the gratitude and affection I feel for them; but prudence forbids it—not that it would harm me, but it might get them into trouble; for it is almost an unpardonable offense to teach slaves to read in this Christian country. It is enough to say of the dear little fellows that they lived on Philpot Street, very near Durgin and Bailey’s ship-yard. I used to talk this matter of slavery over with them. I would sometimes tell them I wished I could be as free as they would be when they grew to be men. “You will be free as soon as you are twenty-one, _but I am a slave for life!_ Have I not as good a right to be free as you do?” These words used to trouble them; they would show me the keenest sympathy and console me with the hope that something would happen by which I might become free.

The boys from whom Douglass obtains reading lessons and practices writing.

## modern-en · anonymous-friend · 11:20

About four months after I came to New Bedford, a young man came to me and asked whether I would like to take the “Liberator.” I told him I would, but having only just escaped from slavery, I said I could not pay for it yet. In the end, though, I did become a subscriber. The paper came, and from week to week I read it with feelings I could not begin to describe. It became my food and my drink. My soul was set on fire. Its sympathy for my brothers in bondage, its scorching condemnations of slaveholders, its honest exposures of slavery, and its powerful attacks on those who upheld the institution sent a thrill of joy through me such as I had never felt before!

The young man who asks Douglass whether he wants to subscribe to the Liberator.

## modern-en · parody-jack · 12:7

“Come, saints and sinners, hear me tell How pious priests whip Jack and Nell, And women buy and children sell, And preach all sinners down to hell, And sing of heavenly union. “They’ll bleat and baa, dona like goats, Gorge down black sheep, and strain at motes, Array their backs in fine black coats, Then seize their negroes by their throats, And choke, for heavenly union. “They’ll church you if you sip a dram, And damn you if you steal a lamb; Yet rob old Tony, Doll, and Sam, Of human rights, and bread and ham; Kidnapper’s heavenly union. “They’ll loudly talk of Christ’s reward, And bind his image with a cord, And scold, and swing the lash abhorred, And sell their brother in the Lord To handcuffed heavenly union. “They’ll read and sing a sacred song, And make a prayer both loud and long, And teach the right and do the wrong, Hailing the brother, sister throng, With words of heavenly union. “We wonder how such saints can sing, Or praise the Lord upon the wing, Who roar, and scold, and whip, and sting, And to their slaves and mammon cling, In guilty conscience union. “They’ll raise tobacco, corn, and rye, And drive, and thieve, and cheat, and lie, And lay up treasures in the sky, By making switch and cowskin fly, In hope of heavenly union. “They’ll crack old Tony on the skull, And preach and roar like Bashan bull, Or braying ass, of mischief full, Then seize old Jacob by the wool, And pull for heavenly union. “A roaring, ranting, sleek man-thief, Who lived on mutton, veal, and beef, Yet never would afford relief To needy, sable sons of grief, Was big with heavenly union. “‘Love not the world,’ the preacher said, And winked his eye, and shook his head; He seized on Tom, and Dick, and Ned, Cut short their meat, and clothes, and bread, Yet still loved heavenly union. “Another preacher whining spoke Of One whose heart for sinners broke: He tied old Nanny to an oak, And drew the blood at every stroke, And prayed for heavenly union. “Two others oped their iron jaws, And waved their children-stealing paws; There sat their children in gewgaws; By stinting negroes’ backs and maws, They kept up heavenly union. “All good from Jack another takes, And entertains their flirts and rakes, Who dress as sleek as glossy snakes, And cram their mouths with sweetened cakes; And this goes down for union.”

An enslaved person named in the appendix’s satirical poem about slaveholding religion.

## modern-en · parody-nell · 12:7

“Come, saints and sinners, hear me tell How pious priests whip Jack and Nell, And women buy and children sell, And preach all sinners down to hell, And sing of heavenly union. “They’ll bleat and baa, dona like goats, Gorge down black sheep, and strain at motes, Array their backs in fine black coats, Then seize their negroes by their throats, And choke, for heavenly union. “They’ll church you if you sip a dram, And damn you if you steal a lamb; Yet rob old Tony, Doll, and Sam, Of human rights, and bread and ham; Kidnapper’s heavenly union. “They’ll loudly talk of Christ’s reward, And bind his image with a cord, And scold, and swing the lash abhorred, And sell their brother in the Lord To handcuffed heavenly union. “They’ll read and sing a sacred song, And make a prayer both loud and long, And teach the right and do the wrong, Hailing the brother, sister throng, With words of heavenly union. “We wonder how such saints can sing, Or praise the Lord upon the wing, Who roar, and scold, and whip, and sting, And to their slaves and mammon cling, In guilty conscience union. “They’ll raise tobacco, corn, and rye, And drive, and thieve, and cheat, and lie, And lay up treasures in the sky, By making switch and cowskin fly, In hope of heavenly union. “They’ll crack old Tony on the skull, And preach and roar like Bashan bull, Or braying ass, of mischief full, Then seize old Jacob by the wool, And pull for heavenly union. “A roaring, ranting, sleek man-thief, Who lived on mutton, veal, and beef, Yet never would afford relief To needy, sable sons of grief, Was big with heavenly union. “‘Love not the world,’ the preacher said, And winked his eye, and shook his head; He seized on Tom, and Dick, and Ned, Cut short their meat, and clothes, and bread, Yet still loved heavenly union. “Another preacher whining spoke Of One whose heart for sinners broke: He tied old Nanny to an oak, And drew the blood at every stroke, And prayed for heavenly union. “Two others oped their iron jaws, And waved their children-stealing paws; There sat their children in gewgaws; By stinting negroes’ backs and maws, They kept up heavenly union. “All good from Jack another takes, And entertains their flirts and rakes, Who dress as sleek as glossy snakes, And cram their mouths with sweetened cakes; And this goes down for union.”

An enslaved person named in the appendix’s satirical poem about slaveholding religion.

## modern-en · darg · 11:6

Thankfully, I did not stay trapped in that misery for long. The kindness of _Mr. David Ruggles_ rescued me from it — a man whose watchfulness, kindness, and persistence I will never forget. I am glad of the chance to express, as far as words can, the love and gratitude I feel toward him. Mr. Ruggles is now stricken with blindness, and is himself in need of the very kindnesses he was once so eager to perform for others. I had been in New York only a few days when Mr. Ruggles sought me out and very kindly took me to his boarding-house at the corner of Church and Lespenard Streets. At the time Mr. Ruggles was deeply caught up in the famous _Darg_ case, while also looking after a number of other fugitive slaves and working out ways and means for their escape; and though he was watched and hemmed in on nearly every side, he seemed more than a match for his enemies.

The enslaver involved in the New York case mentioned in connection with David Ruggles.
